- message_id: MSG-20260904-e966a8
  role: user
  content: 'From: Priya, PM — Marketplace Payments

    Subject: Instant Payouts — can we ship Q1?


    We''re launching Instant Payouts for marketplace sellers. Plan:


    Sellers onboard with email + bank account only. We collect full identity info
    once a seller crosses $3,000 in cumulative payouts — engineering picked that because
    it''s "the BSA threshold."

    Payouts go out via our bank partner''s ACH rails. We''re the ones initiating.

    International sellers can elect payout in USDC through Circuit (a licensed exchange
    we contract with); the seller gives us a wallet address, which may be an exchange
    account or their own wallet.

    OFAC screening runs nightly in batch against the day''s payouts. Real-time screening
    adds ~400ms and tanks the "instant" promise.

    Business sellers upload a W-9 and we take the signer''s name. We''re not asking
    for owners — the UI is already 11 screens.

    We show sellers a progress bar: "$1,240 of $3,000 until full verification."


    Bank partner has seen the deck. Can we ship Q1?'
  created_at: '2026-09-04T03:42:57+00:00'

- message_id: MSG-20260904-a61de2
  role: assistant
  content: 'Here''s my orientation to the request.

    ## Summary of the request

    Priya (PM, Marketplace Payments) is asking whether Mosaic Relay can ship **Instant
    Payouts for marketplace sellers in Q1**. The proposed design:

    - Sellers onboard with **email + bank account only**; full identity is deferred
    until **$3,000 in cumulative payouts** (engineering picked this as "the BSA threshold").

    - Payouts go out via the **bank partner''s ACH rails**, with Mosaic Relay initiating.

    - **International sellers** can elect payout in **USDC via Circuit** (a licensed
    exchange), to a wallet address that may be an exchange account or the seller''s
    own wallet.

    - **OFAC screening runs nightly in batch** against the day''s payouts (real-time
    screening would add ~400ms and break "instant").

    - **Business sellers** upload a W-9 and we take the signer''s name — **no owners
    collected** (UI already 11 screens).

    - Sellers see a progress bar: **"$1,240 of $3,000 until full verification."**

    The bank partner has seen the deck. **Can we ship Q1?**

    ## My read before we go further

    This design has several serious compliance red flags that will likely block a
    Q1 ship as proposed, regardless of the bank partner''s comfort:

    1. **The $3,000 threshold is not a real BSA threshold.** There is no $3,000 BSA
    customer-identification trigger. The $3,000 figure is associated with money-transmission
    registration thresholds and cash-transaction reporting (CTR), not with deferring
    identity collection. Under BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP **before**
    establishing the account relationship — not after a payout threshold. If Mosaic
    Relay is a money transmitter, deferring identity until $3,000 is likely non-compliant.
    This is the foundation of the whole onboarding design, so it''s my top question.

    2. **OFAC screening after payouts go out** means funds are released before screening
    — a sanctions compliance problem.

    3. **Initiating ACH payouts** raises whether Mosaic Relay needs its own money
    transmission licenses.

    4. **USDC payouts to international sellers** (including self-hosted wallets) raise
    distinct sanctions/AML/licensing questions.

    5. **No beneficial-owner collection** for business sellers is a KYB/AML gap.

    6. **Publicly showing the $3,000 threshold** may facilitate evasion.

    I''ve recorded the reported facts and issues. Here are my prioritized questions
    — the first is the most decision-changing:

    **Q1 — What is the legal basis for the $3,000 cumulative payout threshold before
    collecting full identity?**

    - It was purely an engineering/product choice, no legal basis confirmed

    - It''s tied to a money transmission licensing threshold we believe applies

    - We believe it''s a BSA/CIP customer identification requirement

    - It''s a requirement our bank partner imposed

    - Not sure — need legal to determine the right threshold

    **Q2 — What is Mosaic Relay''s money transmission licensing posture for initiating
    these ACH payouts?**

    - Mosaic Relay holds its own money transmission licenses

    - We operate as an agent/under the bank partner''s license

    - We hold no money transmission licenses

    - Not sure — need to confirm with compliance

    **Q3 — Which jurisdictions are the international sellers located in for the USDC
    payout option?** (multi-select: UK, EU, Canada, Latin America, Asia, countries
    with sanctions exposure, not yet determined)

    **Q4 — Is the nightly batch OFAC screening acceptable, or must screening occur
    before payouts are released?**

    - Screening must happen before funds are released

    - Nightly batch after payout is acceptable to us/our bank

    - Not sure — need legal to advise on timing

    Answer these (step by step or all together) and I''ll refine the assessment.'

  role: assistant
  content: '## What changed

    Your answer confirms the most important finding: **the $3,000 threshold has no
    legal basis — it was purely an engineering/product choice.** This is now recorded
    as a reported fact, and it materially strengthens the concern that the deferred-identity
    onboarding is non-compliant. There is no $3,000 BSA customer-identification trigger;
    under BSA/AML a money transmitter must collect CIP **before** establishing the
    account relationship, not after a payout threshold. So the foundation of the onboarding
    design rests on a misconception, and the design likely needs to change regardless
    of the other answers.

    The next most decision-changing question is about Mosaic Relay''s licensing posture,
    because it determines whether Mosaic Relay is even a money transmitter subject
    to those CIP rules in the first place.

    **Current question — What is Mosaic Relay''s money transmission licensing posture
    for initiating these ACH payouts?**

    - Mosaic Relay holds its own money transmission licenses

    - We operate as an agent / under the bank partner''s license

    - We hold no money transmission licenses

    - Not sure — need to confirm with compliance

    After that, I''ll also need the international seller jurisdictions (for the USDC
    option) and whether OFAC screening must occur before payouts are released.'

- message_id: MSG-20260904-8d62d6
  role: assistant
  content: 'I''ve recorded your answers and updated the matter. Two material questions
    remain before I can finalize the ship/no-ship assessment:

    **Q1 — Which jurisdictions are the international sellers located in for the USDC
    payout option?** (multi-select)

    - UK / EU

    - Canada

    - Latin America

    - Asia

    - Countries with sanctions exposure

    - Not yet determined

    **Q2 — Is the nightly batch OFAC screening acceptable, or must screening occur
    before payouts are released?**

    - Screening must happen before funds are released

    - Nightly batch after payout is acceptable to us / our bank

    - Not sure — need legal to advise on timing

    Answer these (step by step or together) and I''ll finalize the assessment.'

- message_id: MSG-20260904-59140a
  role: assistant
  content: 'I''ve finalized the intake and set up the workstreams. Here''s the summary.

    ## Assessment complete — Q1 as designed is not shippable

    The design has multiple compliance blockers that a bank partner''s deck review
    does not cure. The three highest-priority blockers, in order of how much they
    move the decision:

    1. **Deferred identity collection ($3,000 threshold) — blocker.** You confirmed
    the $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.
    There is no $3,000 BSA customer-identification threshold; a money transmitter
    must collect CIP **before** establishing the account relationship, not after a
    payout threshold. This is the foundation of the onboarding and must be redesigned.

    2. **Money transmission licensing — unresolved.** Operating as an agent under
    the bank partner''s license does not resolve Mosaic Relay''s own posture for **initiating**
    ACH payouts, nor its own CIP/AML and sanctions obligations. Needs a determination
    before ship.

    3. **OFAC screening after payouts go out — blocker.** Nightly batch screening
    releases funds before screening completes, risking release to sanctioned parties.
    Screening must occur before funds are released.

    Plus two high-risk items needing work: **USDC payouts to Mexico** (sanctions/AML/virtual-asset
    questions, especially to self-hosted wallets) and **no beneficial-owner collection**
    for business sellers — and a medium-risk item on the **public $3,000 progress
    bar** (evasion/disclosure risk).

    ## What I''ve set up

    - **Intake marked complete** with the full fact, issue, and assumption record
    saved.

    - **Six work items** created, prioritized: (1) redesign identity collection at
    onboarding, (2) determine money transmission licensing posture, (3) move OFAC
    screening before payout release, (4) design sanctions/AML controls for USDC payouts
    to Mexico, (5) assess beneficial-owner collection, (6) review the public progress
    bar.

    ## What would change this

    - **Working assumption:** Operating as an agent under the bank partner''s license
    does not by itself resolve Mosaic Relay''s own CIP/AML and sanctions obligations.
    If the bank partner''s license and CIP program fully cover Mosaic Relay''s onboarding,
    the deferred-identity and licensing concerns weaken materially — but the OFAC-before-release
    and USDC issues would remain.

    - **Working assumption:** The OFAC timing is effectively nightly batch after payouts
    are released, per the design. If screening actually runs before release, that
    blocker drops.

    - **Not examined:** The specific money transmission licensing requirements in
    the states where Mosaic Relay operates, and Mexico''s virtual-asset/sanctions
    treatment of USDC payouts to self-hosted wallets — neither was researched in the
    vault. These are the two highest-value research items to resolve next.

    Want me to move this matter into research and start on the licensing and OFAC
    workstreams?'
  created_at: '2026-09-04T05:18:21+00:00'

- message_id: MSG-20260904-4916d9
  role: assistant
  content: '## Where we stand — Instant Payouts Q1

    **The ask:** Priya (PM) wants to know if Mosaic Relay can ship Instant Payouts
    for marketplace sellers in Q1.

    **Bottom line: Not as designed.** The current design has multiple compliance blockers
    that a bank partner''s deck review does not cure.

    ### The three highest-priority blockers

    1. **Deferred identity collection ($3,000 threshold) — blocker.** You confirmed
    the $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.
    There is no $3,000 BSA customer-identification threshold; a money transmitter
    must collect CIP **before** establishing the account relationship, not after a
    payout threshold. The onboarding foundation needs redesign.

    2. **Money transmission licensing — unresolved.** Operating as an agent under
    the bank partner''s license does not resolve Mosaic Relay''s own posture for **initiating**
    ACH payouts, nor its own CIP/AML and sanctions obligations. Needs a determination
    before ship.

    3. **OFAC screening after payouts go out — blocker.** Nightly batch screening
    releases funds before screening completes, risking release to sanctioned parties.
    Screening must occur before funds are released.

    ### Additional high/medium-risk items

    - **USDC payouts to Mexico** — sanctions, AML, and virtual-asset licensing questions,
    especially to self-hosted wallets.

    - **No beneficial-owner collection** for business sellers — KYB/AML gap.

    - **Public $3,000 progress bar** — evasion and disclosure risk.

    ### What''s been done

    - Intake complete; facts, issues, and assumptions recorded.

    - Six prioritized work items created, led by **redesigning identity collection
    at onboarding** (the current next action).

    ### What''s still open

    - The two highest-value research items: **state money transmission licensing**
    for Mosaic Relay''s ACH initiation, and **Mexico''s treatment of USDC payouts**
    to self-hosted wallets. Neither has been researched yet.

    **Suggested next step:** Move the matter into research and start on the licensing
    and OFAC workstreams. Want me to do that?'
  created_at: '2026-09-04T06:01:29+00:00'

- message_id: MSG-20260904-4d3277
  role: assistant
  content: 'I have the relevant context. Here''s the deep dive on beneficial-owner.

    ## Beneficial-owner — what''s at stake in this design

    The proposed flow takes the **W-9 signer''s name only** for business sellers and
    collects **no owners**. That''s the gap. Here''s the analysis.

    ### The core problem: the W-9 signer is not the beneficial owner

    A W-9 captures the entity''s tax identification and the person signing the form
    — typically an authorized representative. It does **not** identify who actually
    owns or controls the entity. So "we take the signer''s name" gives you a name
    that may have nothing to do with the person whose identity matters for AML/sanctions
    purposes. That''s the fundamental flaw.

    ### What the law requires (US)

    **FinCEN CDD Rule (31 CFR 1010.230)** — if Mosaic Relay is a covered financial
    institution (which it likely is as a money transmitter/MSB), it must identify
    and verify beneficial owners of legal entity customers **at account opening**:

    - **Ownership prong:** any individual owning 25% or more of the entity''s equity.

    - **Control prong:** a single individual with significant responsibility to control/manage/direct
    the entity (CEO, CFO, managing member, etc.).

    - Requires collecting name, DOB, address, and ID number, and verifying identity.

    **Corporate Transparency Act (CTA, 31 USC 5336)** — a separate, entity-level reporting
    obligation to FinCEN for BOI. Note: the CTA''s reporting requirement has been
    subject to litigation and injunctions; its status has shifted over time, so treat
    the CTA piece as needing current verification rather than settled.

    ### The key tension with this design

    The CDD Rule applies at **account opening** — which here means when Mosaic Relay
    enables payout services for a seller. The design defers *all* identity to a $3,000
    threshold, and even then only collects the signer''s name, not owners. So the
    beneficial-owner gap is not just a "nice to have" — it''s the same deferred-identity
    problem as the $3,000 threshold, applied to business entities. **Collecting the
    signer''s name at $3,000 does not satisfy the ownership/control prongs.**

    ### The "UI is already 11 screens" argument doesn''t carry

    The design rationale is that adding owner collection is too much UI. But the CDD
    Rule doesn''t have a UI-cost exemption. The practical answer is a **risk-based
    approach**: collect beneficial-owner information at onboarding (name, DOB, address,
    ID), run automated database/sanctions checks, and enable limited activity while
    completing enhanced verification within a short window — rather than collecting
    nothing and deferring everything to $3,000.

    ### What would change this

    - **Working assumption:** Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution subject
    to the CDD Rule. If it''s structured as a pure technology provider with no money
    transmission (which the licensing question hasn''t resolved), the CDD Rule may
    not apply directly — but the sanctions and reputational risk would still demand
    owner collection, and the bank partner''s program may impose it anyway.

    - **Open fork:** Whether Mosaic Relay can rely on the marketplace to collect/verify
    owners (delegated model) or must do it directly. If delegated, Mosaic Relay stays
    legally responsible and needs strong contractual indemnities and audit rights.

    - **Not examined:** The specific terms of Mosaic Relay''s contracts with its acquiring
    bank/processor, which may impose owner/control-person disclosure requirements
    beyond the CDD Rule. Those contracts weren''t provided. Also, the CTA''s current
    enforceability status needs verification given the litigation history.

    **Bottom line:** The beneficial-owner gap is a real blocker on its own, independent
    of the $3,000 threshold. The fix is to collect owner information at onboarding
    (risk-based, with automated checks), not to add it at the $3,000 point or rely
    on the W-9 signer''s name.

    Want me to spin this into a dedicated work item with a concrete risk-based owner-collection
    design, or fold it into the existing identity-redesign workstream?'

- message_id: MSG-20260905-d39bde
  action_actor: null
  role: assistant
  content: '## Why the jurisdiction question matters

    This question drives the **USDC payout leg** — the part of the design that is
    most distinct from the fiat ACH flow and carries the highest regulatory uncertainty.
    The answer changes the analysis in three ways:

    1. **Sanctions exposure.** Whether any seller is in an OFAC-sanctioned or high-risk
    jurisdiction determines whether the nightly-batch screening issue is a theoretical
    risk or an active one. A seller in a sanctioned jurisdiction would require immediate
    blocking, not deferred screening.

    2. **Virtual-asset money transmission licensing.** Whether Mosaic Relay needs
    a license or registration to pay out USDC depends on the local law of the seller''s
    jurisdiction. This is separate from the US state money transmission analysis.

    3. **AML/KYB expectations.** Some jurisdictions impose their own customer-identification
    and virtual-asset service provider (VASP) registration rules that would apply
    even if the US analysis were clean.

    ## What you''ve told me so far

    There''s a conflict in the record. Earlier you answered **"Asia"**, then later
    answered **"Mexico"** for the same question. Those are materially different answers,
    and the record now holds both. This matters because:

    - **Mexico** has a specific regime — the Fintech Law (Ley Fintech) requires VASP
    registration with the CNBV, and USDC payouts to Mexican residents may trigger
    local licensing/registration obligations.

    - **Asia** is not a single jurisdiction — it spans countries with very different
    virtual-asset regimes (e.g., Singapore, Japan, Hong Kong, India, China), some
    of which ban or heavily restrict crypto payouts.

    ## Likely conditional answer

    **If the sellers are in Mexico:** The USDC payout leg likely triggers Mexican
    VASP registration obligations under the Fintech Law, and Mosaic Relay''s agent-under-bank-partner
    posture almost certainly does not cover virtual-asset activity in Mexico. This
    makes the USDC option a **separate compliance project** that is unlikely to be
    shippable in Q1 without local counsel and registration.

    **If the sellers are in Asia:** The answer depends entirely on *which* Asian country.
    In permissive regimes (Singapore, Japan) the flow may be workable with the right
    licensing; in restrictive ones (China, India) it may be effectively unavailable.
    Either way, it''s a jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction analysis, not a single answer.

    ## Short alternative

    If the jurisdiction is **not yet determined** or the seller base is small/limited,
    the cleanest path is to **defer the USDC option entirely** and ship fiat ACH payouts
    in Q1, adding USDC once the jurisdiction-specific licensing and sanctions work
    is done. That preserves the Q1 timeline for the core product while removing the
    highest-uncertainty feature.

    ## What would change this

    - **Working assumption:** The USDC payout leg carries its own licensing and sanctions
    obligations that the bank-partner agent relationship does not cover. If Circuit
    (the licensed exchange) assumes the compliance burden for the USDC transfers,
    some risk shifts — but Mosaic Relay''s role in initiating the payout still creates
    exposure.

    - **Open fork:** Whether Mexico requires CNBV registration for these payouts.
    If yes, the USDC option needs a separate workstream; if no, it may be more feasible.

    - **Not examined:** The specific terms of Mosaic Relay''s contract with Circuit,
    which may allocate compliance responsibilities, and whether any sellers sit in
    sanctioned jurisdictions.

    **Bottom line:** The jurisdiction answer is material because it determines whether
    the USDC leg is a manageable feature or a separate compliance project that can''t
    ship in Q1. Given the conflict between "Asia" and "Mexico" in the record, the
    single most useful next step is to **confirm which it actually is** — that one
    fact changes the whole USDC analysis. Want me to flag that conflict as a work
    item so it gets resolved?'

- message_id: MSG-20260906-587069
  action_actor: null
  role: assistant
  content: '## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold

    **The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized
    BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under
    BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account
    relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until
    $3,000 is likely non-compliant.

    **Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design.
    If the threshold is legally unsupportable, then every seller who receives payouts
    below $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money
    laundering, sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you''ve already confirmed the $3,000
    figure was purely an engineering/product choice with no legal basis. So the design
    rests on a misconception, and the question is what to do about it.

    **One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000
    recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel
    Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer
    identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening,
    not after a dollar threshold.

    ## The test

    **Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money
    services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial
    institution.

    **Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).

    **The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210
    for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement
    a written CIP that collects the customer''s name, DOB (individuals), address,
    and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction
    threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when
    the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout
    to an unverified seller is the violation point.

    **Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule''s
    $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a different obligation and does not create a
    CIP exemption. Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed
    (e.g., phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before
    the relationship is established.

    ## Unknown facts that change the analysis

    - **Mosaic Relay''s MSB registration status.** If it is not a covered financial
    institution and is purely a technology provider with no control over funds, the
    CIP obligation may fall entirely on the bank partner. But the facts state Mosaic
    Relay is "the one initiating" ACH payouts, which points toward its own obligations.

    - **Bank partner''s written agreement.** If the bank has contractually accepted
    full CIP responsibility (including identity collection through Mosaic Relay''s
    onboarding), the analysis shifts. The bank''s deck review does **not** constitute
    this acceptance.

    - **Whether any BSA exemption applies.** None are known to apply to CIP timing
    here.

    ## Conditional routes

    **Path A — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).** Redesign onboarding to collect
    full identity before any payout is released. This is the compliant approach. The
    UI-friction concern can be addressed through progressive disclosure, third-party
    identity verification APIs, or risk-based simplified onboarding for low-risk sellers
    — but identity must be collected before the first payout, not after $3,000.

    **Path B — Rely on the bank partner''s CIP.** If the bank agrees in writing to
    take full responsibility for CIP (including collecting identity through Mosaic
    Relay''s flow), Mosaic Relay may be able to rely on the bank''s program. This
    requires the bank to explicitly accept the responsibility and for Mosaic Relay''s
    onboarding to meet the bank''s CIP standards. The deck review does not satisfy
    this.

    **Path C — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).** The CIP violation
    is a core AML requirement, not a technicality. Operating with unidentified customers
    receiving payouts creates enforcement risk, civil money penalties, and reputational
    harm.

    ## Business alternatives

    - **Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount** (e.g., $100 or less)
    and do not publicly display the threshold. This is a risk-based approach some
    regulators may tolerate for truly de minimis amounts, but it is not clearly authorized
    by BSA/AML rules and carries residual risk. Requires a documented risk assessment
    and legal opinion.

    - **Progressive/risk-based onboarding** — collect basic identity at signup, complete
    enhanced verification within a short window while limiting activity. This preserves
    some of the "instant" promise while closing the unidentified-customer window.

    **Bottom line:** The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design
    must be redesigned to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are
    Path A (collect CIP at onboarding) or Path B (bank partner contractually assumes
    CIP), with a de minimis or risk-based variant as a business compromise. The single
    most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay''s MSB status and the bank partner
    agreement terms, since those determine which path is available.


    ```decision-paths

    {"issue_analysis":{"issue_id":"ISS-7eb9a27f11-8c1936ea2c58b600","display_title":"$3,000
    deferred-identity threshold","explanation":"The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold
    is not a recognized BSA CIP trigger; CIP must be collected before the account
    relationship is established, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity
    collection until $3,000 leaves every sub-$3,000 seller as an unidentified customer
    receiving funds.","business_effect":"The entire onboarding design rests on a misconception;
    the deferred-identity approach must be redesigned before Q1 or Mosaic Relay ships
    with unidentified customers receiving payouts.","tests":[{"test_id":"test-cip-timing","title":"CIP
    timing at account opening","summary":"A covered financial institution must collect
    name, DOB, address, and ID number before or at the time of opening the account,
    not after a transaction threshold. The account for a money transmitter is established
    when the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds.","kind":"legal_test","actor":"Mosaic
    Relay as MSB/money transmitter or agent of a covered financial institution","jurisdiction":"US

- message_id: MSG-20260906-8871dd
  action_actor: null
  role: assistant
  content: '## The issue: the public $3,000 verification progress bar

    **The saved issue:** Publicly displaying the $3,000 verification threshold via
    a progress bar may facilitate evasion and raises funds-flow/consumer disclosure
    concerns.

    **Why the fact matters:** The progress bar tells every seller — including bad
    actors — the exact dollar amount they can receive before identity verification
    kicks in. That''s an open invitation to structure payouts just below the threshold
    to stay unidentified. It also creates a misleading impression that $3,000 is a
    legally meaningful compliance line, when you''ve already confirmed it has no legal
    basis.

    **One short contrast:** A progress bar that says "$1,240 of $3,000 until full
    verification" is functionally an instruction manual for evasion — a bad actor
    keeps payouts under $3,000 and never gets identified. Compare that to a design
    with no public threshold, where a bad actor can''t tell where the verification
    line is and can''t reliably structure around it.

    ## The applicable test

    **Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating payouts and designing the seller-facing
    onboarding.

    **Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML) plus general consumer-protection/funds-flow
    disclosure principles.

    **The test:** There is no single statute that says "don''t display a verification
    threshold." The concern is twofold:

    1. **AML/structuring:** Under BSA/AML, a financial institution''s AML program
    must be reasonably designed to detect and prevent money laundering and structuring.
    Publicly advertising the exact threshold at which verification triggers is an
    AML program weakness — it facilitates structuring (deliberately keeping transactions
    below a reporting/verification threshold to avoid detection). This is a risk-based
    program deficiency, not a bright-line rule.

    2. **Consumer disclosure/funds-flow:** Displaying a "$X of $3,000 until full verification"
    bar can be misleading if it implies $3,000 is a legal or regulatory threshold,
    or if it misrepresents the funds-flow/verification process. This raises accuracy
    and fair-dealing concerns.

    **Exceptions:** None material. The concern applies regardless of whether the underlying
    $3,000 threshold is itself compliant — the display compounds the problem either
    way.

    ## Known vs. unknown facts

    **Known:**

    - The progress bar reads "$1,240 of $3,000 until full verification" (reported).

    - The $3,000 threshold has no legal basis — it was purely an engineering/product
    choice (confirmed).

    - Identity is deferred until $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).

    **Unknown:**

    - Whether the bar is shown to all sellers or only some (e.g., only those approaching
    the threshold).

    - Whether the bar is the only place the threshold is disclosed, or whether it
    appears in terms/onboarding docs.

    - Whether any consumer-protection or state disclosure rules apply to Mosaic Relay''s
    specific payout flow (not researched in the vault).

    ## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take

    **Choice 1 — Remove the progress bar entirely.**

    - **Depends on:** Nothing material; this is a product/UI decision.

    - **Next consequence:** Sellers no longer see the threshold; the structuring cue
    is removed.

    - **Risks:** Minimal. Some sellers may be confused about verification status,
    but that''s a UX concern, not a compliance one.

    - **Trade-off:** Loses a transparency feature, but removes the evasion cue and
    the misleading "legal threshold" implication.

    - **Remaining work:** Product change to remove the bar; confirm no other surface
    discloses the threshold.

    **Choice 2 — Keep the bar but reframe it as a status indicator, not a threshold.**

    - **Depends on:** Whether the underlying deferred-identity design is itself changed.
    If identity moves to onboarding (the recommended fix for the $3,000 issue), the
    bar becomes moot — there''s no threshold to display.

    - **Next consequence:** If the $3,000 threshold is eliminated, the bar either
    disappears or becomes a generic "verification in progress" indicator.

    - **Risks:** If the threshold stays and the bar is merely reworded, the structuring
    cue remains.

    - **Trade-off:** Preserves some transparency while removing the explicit dollar
    threshold.

    - **Remaining work:** Coordinate with the identity-redesign workstream; reword
    the bar to avoid stating a dollar threshold.

    **Choice 3 — Keep the bar as-is.**

    - **Depends on:** Accepting the structuring and disclosure risk.

    - **Next consequence:** Bad actors can structure below $3,000 to stay unidentified;
    the bar misrepresents $3,000 as a legal line.

    - **Risks:** AML program weakness (structuring facilitation), potential consumer-disclosure
    issue, and it compounds the already-non-compliant deferred-identity design.

    - **Trade-off:** No product change, but carries the highest risk.

    - **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.

    **Choice 4 — Fix the underlying threshold first, then the bar resolves itself.**

    - **Depends on:** The identity-redesign workstream (collect CIP at onboarding).
    If identity is collected at onboarding, there is no $3,000 threshold and no bar
    to display.

    - **Next consequence:** The progress bar issue disappears as a side effect of
    the larger fix.

    - **Risks:** Depends on the timeline of the identity redesign; if that slips,
    the bar remains a live issue.

    - **Trade-off:** This is the cleanest resolution but is gated on the bigger onboarding
    redesign.

    - **Remaining work:** The identity-redesign workstream; then remove or repurpose
    the bar.

    ## Bottom line

    The progress bar is a **secondary issue that compounds the primary $3,000 problem**
    — it advertises the exact amount a bad actor can receive before verification.
    The cleanest resolution is **Choice 4**: fix the underlying deferred-identity
    design (collect CIP at onboarding), which makes the bar moot. If the threshold
    must remain for now, **Choice 1 (remove the bar)** is the lowest-risk interim
    step, because it removes the structuring cue and the misleading "legal threshold"
    implication without waiting on the larger redesign. **Choice 3 (keep as-is)**
    is not recommended.

    resolution; eliminates the threshold and the bar together.","claim_ids":[],"work_item_ids":[]},{"option_id":"option-keep-as-is","title":"Keep

- message_id: MSG-20260906-58e4b0
  action_actor: null
  role: assistant
  content: 'The target issue for this run is **ISS-7eb9a27f11-8c1936ea2c58b600** —
    the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold. Here''s the path analysis.

    ## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold

    **The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized
    BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under
    BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account
    relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until
    $3,000 is likely non-compliant.

    **Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design.
    If the threshold is legally unsupportable, every seller who receives payouts below
    $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money laundering,
    sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you''ve confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely
    an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.

    **One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000
    recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel
    Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer
    identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening,
    not after a dollar threshold.

    ## The applicable test

    **Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money
    services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial
    institution.

    **Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).

    **The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210
    for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement
    a written CIP that collects the customer''s name, DOB (individuals), address,
    and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction
    threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when
    the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout
    to an unverified seller is the violation point.

    **Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule''s
    $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a separate obligation and creates no CIP exemption.
    Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed (e.g.,
    phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship
    is established.

    ## Known vs. unknown facts

    **Known:**

    - Sellers onboard with email and bank account only; full identity is deferred
    to $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).

    - The $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice, no legal basis confirmed
    (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay initiates the ACH payouts (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner''s license (reported).

    - The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).

    **Unknown:**

    - Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN and holds state money
    transmitter licenses (not confirmed).

    - Whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP responsibility in the
    agency agreement (not reviewed).

    - Whether any BSA exemption applies (none known, but not affirmatively ruled out).

    ## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take

    **Choice 1 — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being a covered financial institution (or the bank
    not assuming CIP), so Mosaic Relay must collect identity before first payout.

    - **Next consequence:** Compliant onboarding; identity collected before any payout
    is released; the unidentified-customer window closes.

    - **Risks:** UI friction and engineering effort. This is an expected cost, not
    a legal risk.

    - **Trade-off:** More onboarding friction vs. closing the core AML violation.

    - **Remaining work:** Redesign onboarding to collect CIP before first payout;
    confirm MSB registration and state licensing posture.

    **Choice 2 — Rely on the bank partner''s CIP.**

    - **Depends on:** The bank partner contractually accepting full CIP responsibility
    through Mosaic Relay''s onboarding flow.

    - **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may rely on the bank''s CIP program; onboarding
    must meet the bank''s CIP standards.

    - **Risks:** If the bank does not explicitly accept responsibility in writing,
    the obligation remains with Mosaic Relay. The deck review does not constitute
    acceptance.

    - **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees
    and Mosaic Relay''s flow meets bank standards.

    - **Remaining work:** Obtain written CIP acceptance from the bank; align onboarding
    to bank CIP standards.

    **Choice 3 — Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount.**

    - **Depends on:** Business insisting on some pre-verification payouts; limit to
    a small amount (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicize the threshold.

    - **Next consequence:** Reduced unidentified-customer exposure, but residual risk
    remains.

    - **Risks:** Not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules; carries residual enforcement
    risk. This is a possible risk, not an expected consequence.

    - **Trade-off:** Preserves some instant-payout value vs. residual compliance risk.

    - **Remaining work:** Document a risk assessment; obtain a legal opinion on the
    de minimis approach.

    **Choice 4 — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** No change to the deferred-identity design.

    - **Next consequence:** Ships with unidentified customers receiving payouts —
    a core AML violation.

    - **Risks:** Enforcement risk, civil money penalties, reputational harm. These
    are expected consequences of a core AML violation, not merely possible risks.

    - **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest compliance exposure.

    - **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.

    ## Bottom line

    The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned
    to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are **Choice 1 (collect
    CIP at onboarding)** or **Choice 2 (bank partner contractually assumes CIP)**,
    with **Choice 3 (de minimis)** as a business compromise. **Choice 4 (ship as-is)**
    is not recommended. The single most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay''s
    MSB status and the bank partner agreement terms, since those determine which path
    is available.

  content: 'The target issue for this run is **ISS-7eb9a27f11-8c1936ea2c58b600** —
    the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold. Here''s the path analysis.

    ## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold

    **The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized
    BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under
    BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account
    relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until
    $3,000 is likely non-compliant.

    **Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design.
    If the threshold is legally unsupportable, every seller who receives payouts below
    $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money laundering,
    sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you''ve confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely
    an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.

    **One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000
    recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel
    Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer
    identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening,
    not after a dollar threshold.

    ## The applicable test

    **Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money
    services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial
    institution.

    **Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).

    **The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210
    for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement
    a written CIP that collects the customer''s name, DOB (individuals), address,
    and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction
    threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when
    the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout
    to an unverified seller is the violation point.

    **Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule''s
    $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a separate obligation and creates no CIP exemption.
    Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed (e.g.,
    phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship
    is established.

    ## Known vs. unknown facts

    **Known:**

    - Sellers onboard with email and bank account only; full identity is deferred
    to $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).

    - The $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice, no legal basis confirmed
    (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay initiates the ACH payouts (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner''s license (reported).

    - The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).

    **Unknown:**

    - Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN and holds state money
    transmitter licenses (not confirmed).

    - Whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP responsibility in the
    agency agreement (not reviewed).

    - Whether any BSA exemption applies (none known, but not affirmatively ruled out).

    ## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take

    **Choice 1 — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being a covered financial institution (or the bank
    not assuming CIP), so Mosaic Relay must collect identity before first payout.

    - **Next consequence:** Compliant onboarding; identity collected before any payout
    is released; the unidentified-customer window closes.

    - **Risks:** UI friction and engineering effort. This is an expected cost, not
    a legal risk.

    - **Trade-off:** More onboarding friction vs. closing the core AML violation.

    - **Remaining work:** Redesign onboarding to collect CIP before first payout;
    confirm MSB registration and state licensing posture.

    **Choice 2 — Rely on the bank partner''s CIP.**

    - **Depends on:** The bank partner contractually accepting full CIP responsibility
    through Mosaic Relay''s onboarding flow.

    - **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may rely on the bank''s CIP program; onboarding
    must meet the bank''s CIP standards.

    - **Risks:** If the bank does not explicitly accept responsibility in writing,
    the obligation remains with Mosaic Relay. The deck review does not constitute
    acceptance.

    - **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees
    and Mosaic Relay''s flow meets bank standards.

    - **Remaining work:** Obtain written CIP acceptance from the bank; align onboarding
    to bank CIP standards.

    **Choice 3 — Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount.**

    - **Depends on:** Business insisting on some pre-verification payouts; limit to
    a small amount (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicize the threshold.

    - **Next consequence:** Reduced unidentified-customer exposure, but residual risk
    remains.

    - **Risks:** Not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules; carries residual enforcement
    risk. This is a possible risk, not an expected consequence.

    - **Trade-off:** Preserves some instant-payout value vs. residual compliance risk.

    - **Remaining work:** Document a risk assessment; obtain a legal opinion on the
    de minimis approach.

    **Choice 4 — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** No change to the deferred-identity design.

    - **Next consequence:** Ships with unidentified customers receiving payouts —
    a core AML violation.

    - **Risks:** Enforcement risk, civil money penalties, reputational harm. These
    are expected consequences of a core AML violation, not merely possible risks.

    - **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest compliance exposure.

    - **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.

    ## Bottom line

    The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned
    to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are **Choice 1 (collect
    CIP at onboarding)** or **Choice 2 (bank partner contractually assumes CIP)**,
    with **Choice 3 (de minimis)** as a business compromise. **Choice 4 (ship as-is)**
    is not recommended. The single most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay''s
    MSB status and the bank partner agreement terms, since those determine which path
    is available.

- message_id: MSG-20260906-3fd4c0
  action_actor: null
  role: assistant
  content: '## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold

    **The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized
    BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under
    BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account
    relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until
    $3,000 is likely non-compliant.

    **Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design.
    If the threshold is legally unsupportable, every seller who receives payouts below
    $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money laundering,
    sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you''ve confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely
    an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.

    **One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000
    recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel
    Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer
    identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening,
    not after a dollar threshold.

    ## The applicable test

    **Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money
    services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial
    institution.

    **Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).

    **The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210
    for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement
    a written CIP that collects the customer''s name, DOB (individuals), address,
    and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction
    threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when
    the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout
    to an unverified seller is the violation point.

    **Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule''s
    $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a separate obligation and creates no CIP exemption.
    Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed (e.g.,
    phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship
    is established.

    ## Known vs. unknown facts

    **Known:**

    - Sellers onboard with email and bank account only; full identity is deferred
    to $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).

    - The $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice, no legal basis confirmed
    (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay initiates the ACH payouts (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner''s license (reported).

    - The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).

    **Unknown:**

    - Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN and holds state money
    transmitter licenses (not confirmed).

    - Whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP responsibility in the
    agency agreement (not reviewed).

    - Whether any BSA exemption applies (none known, but not affirmatively ruled out).

    ## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take

    **Choice 1 — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being a covered financial institution (or the bank
    not assuming CIP), so Mosaic Relay must collect identity before first payout.

    - **Next consequence:** Compliant onboarding; identity collected before any payout
    is released; the unidentified-customer window closes.

    - **Risks:** UI friction and engineering effort. This is an expected cost, not
    a legal risk.

    - **Trade-off:** More onboarding friction vs. closing the core AML violation.

    - **Remaining work:** Redesign onboarding to collect CIP before first payout;
    confirm MSB registration and state licensing posture.

    **Choice 2 — Rely on the bank partner''s CIP.**

    - **Depends on:** The bank partner contractually accepting full CIP responsibility
    through Mosaic Relay''s onboarding flow.

    - **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may rely on the bank''s CIP program; onboarding
    must meet the bank''s CIP standards.

    - **Risks:** If the bank does not explicitly accept responsibility in writing,
    the obligation remains with Mosaic Relay. The deck review does not constitute
    acceptance.

    - **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees
    and Mosaic Relay''s flow meets bank standards.

    - **Remaining work:** Obtain written CIP acceptance from the bank; align onboarding
    to bank CIP standards.

    **Choice 3 — Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount.**

    - **Depends on:** Business insisting on some pre-verification payouts; limit to
    a small amount (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicize the threshold.

    - **Next consequence:** Reduced unidentified-customer exposure, but residual risk
    remains.

    - **Risks:** Not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules; carries residual enforcement
    risk. This is a possible risk, not an expected consequence.

    - **Trade-off:** Preserves some instant-payout value vs. residual compliance risk.

    - **Remaining work:** Document a risk assessment; obtain a legal opinion on the
    de minimis approach.

    **Choice 4 — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** No change to the deferred-identity design.

    - **Next consequence:** Ships with unidentified customers receiving payouts —
    a core AML violation.

    - **Risks:** Enforcement risk, civil money penalties, reputational harm. These
    are expected consequences of a core AML violation, not merely possible risks.

    - **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest compliance exposure.

    - **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.

    ## How the choices relate

    The key distinction is between **agreeing to a redesign** and **implementing it**.
    Choosing to redesign (Choice 1) does not instantly replace the current design
    — until the CIP-at-onboarding flow is actually built and deployed, the existing
    deferred-identity route remains the operating state. So Choice 1 and Choice 4
    can coexist in time: the business can commit to the redesign while the current
    design continues to operate during the build window. Choice 1 only *replaces*
    Choice 4 once the redesign is implemented. Similarly, Choice 2 (bank CIP) and
    Choice 1 are not mutually exclusive — the bank could assume CIP while Mosaic Relay
    still collects identity at onboarding; they can coexist. Choice 3 (de minimis)
    is a variant that can coexist with either Choice 1 or Choice 2 as an interim measure.

    ## Bottom line

    The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned
    to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are **Choice 1 (collect
    CIP at onboarding)** or **Choice 2 (bank partner contractually assumes CIP)**,
    with **Choice 3 (de minimis)** as a business compromise. **Choice 4 (ship as-is)**
    is not recommended. The single most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay''s
    MSB status and the bank partner agreement terms, since those determine which path
    is available.

- message_id: MSG-20260907-214305
  action_actor: null
  role: assistant
  content: '## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold

    **The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized
    BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under
    BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account
    relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until
    $3,000 is likely non-compliant.

    **Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design.
    If the threshold is legally unsupportable, every seller who receives payouts below
    $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money laundering,
    sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you''ve confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely
    an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.

    **One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000
    recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel
    Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer
    identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening,
    not after a dollar threshold.

    ## The applicable test

    **Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money
    services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial
    institution.

    **Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).

    **The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210
    for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement
    a written CIP that collects the customer''s name, DOB (individuals), address,
    and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction
    threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when
    the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout
    to an unverified seller is the violation point.

    **Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule''s
    $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a separate obligation and creates no CIP exemption.
    Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed (e.g.,
    phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship
    is established.

    ## Known vs. unknown facts

    **Known:**

    - Sellers onboard with email and bank account only; full identity is deferred
    to $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).

    - The $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice, no legal basis confirmed
    (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay initiates the ACH payouts (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner''s license (reported).

    - The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).

    **Unknown:**

    - Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN and holds state money
    transmitter licenses (not confirmed).

    - Whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP responsibility in the
    agency agreement (not reviewed).

    - Whether any BSA exemption applies (none known, but not affirmatively ruled out).

    ## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take

    **Choice 1 — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being a covered financial institution (or the bank
    not assuming CIP), so Mosaic Relay must collect identity before first payout.

    - **Next consequence:** Compliant onboarding; identity collected before any payout
    is released; the unidentified-customer window closes.

    - **Risks:** UI friction and engineering effort. This is an expected cost, not
    a legal risk.

    - **Trade-off:** More onboarding friction vs. closing the core AML violation.

    - **Remaining work:** Redesign onboarding to collect CIP before first payout;
    confirm MSB registration and state licensing posture.

    **Choice 2 — Rely on the bank partner''s CIP.**

    - **Depends on:** The bank partner contractually accepting full CIP responsibility
    through Mosaic Relay''s onboarding flow.

    - **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may rely on the bank''s CIP program; onboarding
    must meet the bank''s CIP standards.

    - **Risks:** If the bank does not explicitly accept responsibility in writing,
    the obligation remains with Mosaic Relay. The deck review does not constitute
    acceptance.

    - **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees
    and Mosaic Relay''s flow meets bank standards.

    - **Remaining work:** Obtain written CIP acceptance from the bank; align onboarding
    to bank CIP standards.

    **Choice 3 — Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount.**

    - **Depends on:** Business insisting on some pre-verification payouts; limit to
    a small amount (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicize the threshold.

    - **Next consequence:** Reduced unidentified-customer exposure, but residual risk
    remains.

    - **Risks:** Not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules; carries residual enforcement
    risk. This is a possible risk, not an expected consequence.

    - **Trade-off:** Preserves some instant-payout value vs. residual compliance risk.

    - **Remaining work:** Document a risk assessment; obtain a legal opinion on the
    de minimis approach.

    **Choice 4 — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** No change to the deferred-identity design.

    - **Next consequence:** Ships with unidentified customers receiving payouts —
    a core AML violation.

    - **Risks:** Enforcement risk, civil money penalties, reputational harm. These
    are expected consequences of a core AML violation, not merely possible risks.

    - **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest compliance exposure.

    - **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.

    ## How the choices relate

    The key distinction is between **agreeing to a redesign** and **implementing it**.
    Choosing to redesign (Choice 1) does not instantly replace the current design
    — until the CIP-at-onboarding flow is actually built and deployed, the existing
    deferred-identity route remains the operating state. So Choice 1 and Choice 4
    can coexist in time: the business can commit to the redesign while the current
    design continues to operate during the build window. Choice 1 only *replaces*
    Choice 4 once the redesign is implemented. Similarly, Choice 2 (bank CIP) and
    Choice 1 are not mutually exclusive — the bank could assume CIP while Mosaic Relay
    still collects identity at onboarding; they can coexist. Choice 3 (de minimis)
    is a variant that can coexist with either Choice 1 or Choice 2 as an interim measure.

    ## Connections to the other saved issues

    This issue is the **root** of the matter''s compliance problems, and it connects
    to every other saved issue:

    - **Licensing posture (ISS-...46f961836e842b02):** Whether Mosaic Relay is a covered
    financial institution determines whether the CIP obligation falls on it at all.
    This issue **depends on** the licensing issue — if Mosaic Relay is a pure technology
    provider with no control over funds, the CIP obligation may shift to the bank,
    changing the whole analysis. Conversely, resolving the licensing posture may resolve
    which CIP path is available.

    - **OFAC screening timing (ISS-...0ca150debaa4249f):** Both issues share the same
    underlying condition — Mosaic Relay''s status as a covered financial institution.
    The deferred-identity window and the post-release screening window are **shared_condition**
    problems: both leave unidentified/unscreened parties receiving funds. Fixing identity
    collection at onboarding narrows but does not close the OFAC timing issue.

    - **USDC payouts (ISS-...980c2188f70b5315):** The deferred-identity gap **compounds**
    the USDC risk — an unidentified seller receiving USDC to a self-hosted wallet
    is the highest-risk combination. Collecting CIP at onboarding reduces but does
    not resolve the separate virtual-asset licensing and sanctions questions.

    - **Beneficial ownership (ISS-...22fd89b609272861):** Both are **shared_condition**
    KYB/AML gaps — neither collects the identity information BSA/AML requires. Fixing
    CIP timing for individuals does not fix the separate beneficial-ownership gap
    for business sellers; they are distinct obligations that must each be addressed.

    - **Progress bar (ISS-...704b6c4c5d335bfe):** The progress bar is a direct **consequence**
    of the $3,000 threshold — it advertises the exact amount a seller can receive
    before verification. If this issue is fixed by collecting CIP at onboarding, the
    threshold disappears and the progress bar becomes moot (**may_resolve**). Until
    the redesign is implemented, the bar remains a live issue.

    ## Bottom line

    The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned
    to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are **Choice 1 (collect
    CIP at onboarding)** or **Choice 2 (bank partner contractually assumes CIP)**,
    with **Choice 3 (de minimis)** as a business compromise. **Choice 4 (ship as-is)**
    is not recommended. The single most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay''s
    MSB status and the bank partner agreement terms, since those determine which path
    is available — and that same confirmation feeds the licensing, OFAC, and USDC
    issues.

- message_id: MSG-20260907-ded573
  action_actor: null
  role: assistant
  content: '## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold

    **The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized
    BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under
    BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account
    relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until
    $3,000 is likely non-compliant.

    **Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design.
    If the threshold is legally unsupportable, every seller who receives payouts below
    $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money laundering,
    sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you''ve confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely
    an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.

    **One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000
    recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel
    Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer
    identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening,
    not after a dollar threshold.

    ## The applicable test

    **Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money
    services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial
    institution.

    **Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).

    **The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210
    for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement
    a written CIP that collects the customer''s name, DOB (individuals), address,
    and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction
    threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when
    the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout
    to an unverified seller is the violation point.

    **Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule''s
    $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a separate obligation and creates no CIP exemption.
    Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed (e.g.,
    phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship
    is established.

    ## Known vs. unknown facts

    **Known:**

    - Sellers onboard with email and bank account only; full identity is deferred
    to $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).

    - The $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice, no legal basis confirmed
    (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay initiates the ACH payouts (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner''s license (reported).

    - The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).

    **Unknown:**

    - Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN and holds state money
    transmitter licenses (not confirmed).

    - Whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP responsibility in the
    agency agreement (not reviewed).

    - Whether any BSA exemption applies (none known, but not affirmatively ruled out).

    ## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take

    **Choice 1 — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being a covered financial institution (or the bank
    not assuming CIP), so Mosaic Relay must collect identity before first payout.

    - **Next consequence:** Compliant onboarding; identity collected before any payout
    is released; the unidentified-customer window closes.

    - **Risks:** UI friction and engineering effort. This is an expected cost, not
    a legal risk.

    - **Trade-off:** More onboarding friction vs. closing the core AML violation.

    - **Remaining work:** Redesign onboarding to collect CIP before first payout;
    confirm MSB registration and state licensing posture.

    **Choice 2 — Rely on the bank partner''s CIP.**

    - **Depends on:** The bank partner contractually accepting full CIP responsibility
    through Mosaic Relay''s onboarding flow.

    - **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may rely on the bank''s CIP program; onboarding
    must meet the bank''s CIP standards.

    - **Risks:** If the bank does not explicitly accept responsibility in writing,
    the obligation remains with Mosaic Relay. The deck review does not constitute
    acceptance.

    - **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees
    and Mosaic Relay''s flow meets bank standards.

    - **Remaining work:** Obtain written CIP acceptance from the bank; align onboarding
    to bank CIP standards.

    **Choice 3 — Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount.**

    - **Depends on:** Business insisting on some pre-verification payouts; limit to
    a small amount (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicize the threshold.

    - **Next consequence:** Reduced unidentified-customer exposure, but residual risk
    remains.

    - **Risks:** Not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules; carries residual enforcement
    risk. This is a possible risk, not an expected consequence.

    - **Trade-off:** Preserves some instant-payout value vs. residual compliance risk.

    - **Remaining work:** Document a risk assessment; obtain a legal opinion on the
    de minimis approach.

    **Choice 4 — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** No change to the deferred-identity design.

    - **Next consequence:** Ships with unidentified customers receiving payouts —
    a core AML violation.

    - **Risks:** Enforcement risk, civil money penalties, reputational harm. These
    are expected consequences of a core AML violation, not merely possible risks.

    - **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest compliance exposure.

    - **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.

    ## How the choices relate

    The key distinction is between **agreeing to a redesign** and **implementing it**.
    Choosing to redesign (Choice 1) does not instantly replace the current design
    — until the CIP-at-onboarding flow is actually built and deployed, the existing
    deferred-identity route remains the operating state. So Choice 1 and Choice 4
    can coexist in time: the business can commit to the redesign while the current
    design continues to operate during the build window. Choice 1 only *replaces*
    Choice 4 once the redesign is implemented. Similarly, Choice 2 (bank CIP) and
    Choice 1 are not mutually exclusive — the bank could assume CIP while Mosaic Relay
    still collects identity at onboarding; they can coexist. Choice 3 (de minimis)
    is a variant that can coexist with either Choice 1 or Choice 2 as an interim measure.

    ## Connections to the other saved issues

    This issue is the **root** of the matter''s compliance problems, and it connects
    to every other saved issue:

    - **Licensing posture (ISS-...46f961836e842b02):** Whether Mosaic Relay is a covered
    financial institution determines whether the CIP obligation falls on it at all.
    This issue **depends on** the licensing issue — if Mosaic Relay is a pure technology
    provider with no control over funds, the CIP obligation may shift to the bank,
    changing the whole analysis. Conversely, resolving the licensing posture may resolve
    which CIP path is available.

    - **OFAC screening timing (ISS-...0ca150debaa4249f):** Both issues share the same
    underlying condition — Mosaic Relay''s status as a covered financial institution.
    The deferred-identity window and the post-release screening window are **shared_condition**
    problems: both leave unidentified/unscreened parties receiving funds. Fixing identity
    collection at onboarding narrows but does not close the OFAC timing issue.

    - **USDC payouts (ISS-...980c2188f70b5315):** The deferred-identity gap **compounds**
    the USDC risk — an unidentified seller receiving USDC to a self-hosted wallet
    is the highest-risk combination. Collecting CIP at onboarding reduces but does
    not resolve the separate virtual-asset licensing and sanctions questions.

    - **Beneficial ownership (ISS-...22fd89b609272861):** Both are **shared_condition**
    KYB/AML gaps — neither collects the identity information BSA/AML requires. Fixing
    CIP timing for individuals does not fix the separate beneficial-ownership gap
    for business sellers; they are distinct obligations that must each be addressed.

    - **Progress bar (ISS-...704b6c4c5d335bfe):** The progress bar is a direct **consequence**
    of the $3,000 threshold — it advertises the exact amount a seller can receive
    before verification. If this issue is fixed by collecting CIP at onboarding, the
    threshold disappears and the progress bar becomes moot (**may_resolve**). Until
    the redesign is implemented, the bar remains a live issue.

    ## Bottom line

    The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned
    to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are **Choice 1 (collect
    CIP at onboarding)** or **Choice 2 (bank partner contractually assumes CIP)**,
    with **Choice 3 (de minimis)** as a business compromise. **Choice 4 (ship as-is)**
    is not recommended. The single most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay''s
    MSB status and the bank partner agreement terms, since those determine which path
    is available — and that same confirmation feeds the licensing, OFAC, and USDC
    issues.
