- message_id: MSG-20260907-be7c9e
  action_actor: null
  role: assistant
  content: 'The target issue for this run is **ISS-7eb9a27f11-46f961836e842b02** —
    Mosaic Relay''s money transmission licensing posture. Here''s the path analysis.

    ## The issue: money transmission licensing posture

    **The saved issue:** Operating as an agent under the bank partner''s license does
    not resolve Mosaic Relay''s own money transmission licensing posture for initiating
    ACH payouts, nor its own CIP/AML and sanctions obligations.

    **Why the fact matters:** This is the threshold question that determines whether
    Mosaic Relay can lawfully initiate payouts at all — and it gates every other issue
    in the matter. If Mosaic Relay is itself a money transmitter (or is deemed to
    be one), it needs its own state money transmitter licenses and its own FinCEN
    MSB registration, and it bears its own CIP/AML and sanctions obligations. If it
    is a pure technology provider with no control over funds, those obligations may
    shift to the bank partner. The agent-under-the-bank''s-license framing is not
    a legal determination — it''s a business arrangement that must be tested against
    each state''s money transmitter act.

    **One short contrast:** "We operate as an agent under the bank partner''s license"
    sounds like it resolves the licensing question, but it doesn''t. In many states,
    an "agent of a licensee" exemption has specific conditions — the agent must not
    receive, hold, or control funds, and the principal must be responsible for the
    agent''s acts. If Mosaic Relay is the party *initiating* the ACH entries and controlling
    the funds flow, it may not qualify for the exemption and may need its own license.
    The bank partner "seeing the deck" is not a legal determination of Mosaic Relay''s
    own obligations.

    ## The applicable test

    **Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts.

    **Jurisdiction:** State-by-state (each state''s money transmitter act) plus federal
    (FinCEN MSB registration).

    **The test:** Money transmission licensing is a state-by-state regime. The key
    questions are: (1) whether initiating ACH payouts falls within the state''s definition
    of "money transmission," and (2) whether Mosaic Relay qualifies for an exemption
    as an agent of a licensed bank or agent of an exempt person. The critical factual
    driver is whether Mosaic Relay ever **receives, holds, or controls funds** in
    the payout flow, or whether it acts purely as a third-party sender instructing
    the bank partner. If Mosaic Relay controls the funds flow, it likely needs its
    own licenses.

    **Exceptions:** Agent-of-a-licensee exemptions exist in many states but are conditional
    — they typically require the agent to not hold or control funds and the principal
    to be responsible. Whether Mosaic Relay qualifies depends on the specific statute
    in each state where it operates.

    ## Known vs. unknown facts

    **Known:**

    - Mosaic Relay initiates the ACH payouts (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner''s license (reported).

    - The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).

    **Unknown:**

    - Whether Mosaic Relay ever receives, holds, or controls funds in the payout flow
    (not confirmed).

    - Which specific states Mosaic Relay operates in and where sellers are located
    (not confirmed).

    - The specific terms of the agency agreement with the bank partner (not reviewed).

    - Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN (not confirmed).

    - Whether the agent-of-a-licensee exemption applies in the relevant states (not
    confirmed).

    ## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take

    **Choice 1 — Confirm the funds-flow and agency-agreement facts (recommended first
    step).**

    - **Depends on:** Determining whether Mosaic Relay receives, holds, or controls
    funds, and reviewing the agency agreement terms.

    - **Next consequence:** Establishes whether Mosaic Relay is a money transmitter
    needing its own licenses, or qualifies for an agent exemption.

    - **Risks:** None — this is fact-gathering, not a decision.

    - **Trade-off:** Requires internal and legal review before any launch decision.

    - **Remaining work:** Review the agency agreement; map the funds flow; identify
    the states involved.

    **Choice 2 — Obtain state money transmitter licenses.**

    - **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being deemed a money transmitter in the relevant
    states (i.e., it controls funds or doesn''t qualify for an exemption).

    - **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay can lawfully initiate payouts in those states;
    it also becomes a covered financial institution with its own CIP/AML and sanctions
    obligations.

    - **Risks:** Licensing is time-consuming and state-by-state; may not be achievable
    by Q1. This is an expected cost/constraint, not a legal risk.

    - **Trade-off:** Full compliance vs. significant time and cost that may miss Q1.

    - **Remaining work:** Identify states, prepare applications, obtain licenses,
    register as an MSB with FinCEN.

    **Choice 3 — Restructure so the bank partner is the party initiating payouts.**

    - **Depends on:** The bank partner agreeing to be the ODFI/initiating party, with
    Mosaic Relay as a pure technology provider that never controls funds.

    - **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may qualify for an agent exemption and avoid
    its own licensing; the bank bears primary CIP/AML and sanctions obligations.

    - **Risks:** Requires the bank to accept this role contractually; the bank may
    not agree. The deck review does not constitute this acceptance.

    - **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees
    and the funds flow is restructured.

    - **Remaining work:** Renegotiate the agency agreement; restructure the funds
    flow; obtain written confirmation from the bank.

    **Choice 4 — Launch only in states where an exemption is confirmed.**

    - **Depends on:** Identifying states where Mosaic Relay qualifies for an agent-of-a-licensee
    exemption.

    - **Next consequence:** A Q1 launch is possible but only in a reduced set of states.

    - **Risks:** Reduced addressable market; requires confirming each state''s exemption
    conditions.

    - **Trade-off:** Preserves some Q1 launch vs. reduced market coverage.

    - **Remaining work:** State-by-state exemption analysis.

    **Choice 5 — Ship as-is and accept the licensing risk (not recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** No change to the current agent-under-the-bank''s-license arrangement.

    - **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay initiates payouts without confirming its
    own licensing posture.

    - **Risks:** Unlicensed money transmission enforcement risk, fines, and reputational
    harm. This is an expected consequence if Mosaic Relay is deemed a money transmitter,
    not merely a possible risk.

    - **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest licensing exposure.

    - **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.

    ## How the choices relate

    The key distinction is between **agreeing to a restructure** and **implementing
    it**. Choosing Choice 3 (restructure so the bank initiates) does not instantly
    change the operating state — until the agency agreement is renegotiated and the
    funds flow is actually restructured, the current arrangement remains. So Choice
    3 and Choice 5 can coexist in time: the business can commit to the restructure
    while the current arrangement continues during the transition. Choice 3 only *replaces*
    Choice 5 once the restructure is implemented. Similarly, Choice 2 (obtain licenses)
    and Choice 3 (restructure) are not mutually exclusive — Mosaic Relay could obtain
    licenses in some states while restructuring in others; they can coexist.

    ## Connections to the other saved issues

    This licensing issue is the **gateway** to the matter''s other compliance issues,
    because it determines whether Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution:

    - **$3,000 threshold (ISS-...8c1936ea2c58b600):** This issue **may_resolve** the
    threshold issue — if Mosaic Relay is a pure technology provider with no control
    over funds, the CIP obligation may shift to the bank, changing the whole deferred-identity
    analysis. Conversely, if Mosaic Relay is a money transmitter, it bears its own
    CIP obligation and the $3,000 threshold is non-compliant.

    - **OFAC screening timing (ISS-...0ca150debaa4249f):** **shared_condition** —
    both depend on Mosaic Relay''s status as a covered financial institution. If Mosaic
    Relay is a money transmitter, it bears its own sanctions obligations; if not,
    they may shift to the bank.

    - **USDC payouts (ISS-...980c2188f70b5315):** **compounds** — the licensing question
    is more acute for USDC payouts, which may require separate virtual-asset money
    transmission licenses beyond the fiat ACH analysis. Resolving the fiat licensing
    posture does not resolve the USDC licensing question.

    - **Beneficial ownership (ISS-...22fd89b609272861):** **shared_condition** — if
    Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution, the KYB/beneficial-ownership
    obligations apply to it directly; if not, they may fall on the bank.

    - **Progress bar (ISS-...704b6c4c5d335bfe):** **depends_on** — the progress bar''s
    consumer-disclosure concern is secondary to whether Mosaic Relay is a covered
    financial institution at all; the licensing posture determines the framework within
    which the disclosure concern is assessed.

    ## Bottom line

    The licensing posture is the **gateway issue** — it determines whether Mosaic
    Relay is a covered financial institution and therefore whether it bears its own
    CIP/AML, sanctions, and KYB obligations. The single most useful next step is **Choice
    1**: confirm the funds-flow facts and review the agency agreement, because that
    determines whether Mosaic Relay needs its own licenses (Choice 2), can restructure
    so the bank initiates (Choice 3), or can launch in a limited set of states (Choice
    4). **Choice 5 (ship as-is)** is not recommended.

- message_id: MSG-20260907-0d6451
  action_actor: null
  role: assistant
  content: 'The target issue for this run is **ISS-7eb9a27f11-8c1936ea2c58b600** —
    the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold. Here''s the path analysis.

    ## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold

    **The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized
    BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under
    BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account
    relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until
    $3,000 is likely non-compliant.

    **Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design.
    If the threshold is legally unsupportable, every seller who receives payouts below
    $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money laundering,
    sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you''ve confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely
    an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.

    **One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000
    recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel
    Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer
    identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening,
    not after a dollar threshold.

    ## The applicable test

    **Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money
    services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial
    institution.

    **Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).

    **The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210
    for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement
    a written CIP that collects the customer''s name, DOB (individuals), address,
    and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction
    threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when
    the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout
    to an unverified seller is the violation point.

    **Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule''s
    $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a separate obligation and creates no CIP exemption.
    Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed (e.g.,
    phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship
    is established.

    ## Known vs. unknown facts

    **Known:**

    - Sellers onboard with email and bank account only; full identity is deferred
    to $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).

    - The $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice, no legal basis confirmed
    (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay initiates the ACH payouts (reported).

    - Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner''s license (reported).

    - The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).

    **Unknown:**

    - Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN and holds state money
    transmitter licenses (not confirmed).

    - Whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP responsibility in the
    agency agreement (not reviewed).

    - Whether any BSA exemption applies (none known, but not affirmatively ruled out).

    ## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take

    **Choice 1 — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being a covered financial institution (or the bank
    not assuming CIP), so Mosaic Relay must collect identity before first payout.

    - **Next consequence:** Compliant onboarding; identity collected before any payout
    is released; the unidentified-customer window closes.

    - **Risks:** UI friction and engineering effort. This is an expected cost, not
    a legal risk.

    - **Trade-off:** More onboarding friction vs. closing the core AML violation.

    - **Remaining work:** Redesign onboarding to collect CIP before first payout;
    confirm MSB registration and state licensing posture.

    **Choice 2 — Rely on the bank partner''s CIP.**

    - **Depends on:** The bank partner contractually accepting full CIP responsibility
    through Mosaic Relay''s onboarding flow.

    - **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may rely on the bank''s CIP program; onboarding
    must meet the bank''s CIP standards.

    - **Risks:** If the bank does not explicitly accept responsibility in writing,
    the obligation remains with Mosaic Relay. The deck review does not constitute
    acceptance.

    - **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees
    and Mosaic Relay''s flow meets bank standards.

    - **Remaining work:** Obtain written CIP acceptance from the bank; align onboarding
    to bank CIP standards.

    **Choice 3 — Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount.**

    - **Depends on:** Business insisting on some pre-verification payouts; limit to
    a small amount (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicize the threshold.

    - **Next consequence:** Reduced unidentified-customer exposure, but residual risk
    remains.

    - **Risks:** Not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules; carries residual enforcement
    risk. This is a possible risk, not an expected consequence.

    - **Trade-off:** Preserves some instant-payout value vs. residual compliance risk.

    - **Remaining work:** Document a risk assessment; obtain a legal opinion on the
    de minimis approach.

    **Choice 4 — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).**

    - **Depends on:** No change to the deferred-identity design.

    - **Next consequence:** Ships with unidentified customers receiving payouts —
    a core AML violation.

    - **Risks:** Enforcement risk, civil money penalties, reputational harm. These
    are expected consequences of a core AML violation, not merely possible risks.

    - **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest compliance exposure.

    - **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.

    ## How the choices relate

    The key distinction is between **agreeing to a redesign** and **implementing it**.
    Choosing to redesign (Choice 1) does not instantly replace the current design
    — until the CIP-at-onboarding flow is actually built and deployed, the existing
    deferred-identity route remains the operating state. So Choice 1 and Choice 4
    can coexist in time: the business can commit to the redesign while the current
    design continues to operate during the build window. Choice 1 only *replaces*
    Choice 4 once the redesign is implemented. Similarly, Choice 2 (bank CIP) and
    Choice 1 are not mutually exclusive — the bank could assume CIP while Mosaic Relay
    still collects identity at onboarding; they can coexist. Choice 3 (de minimis)
    is a variant that can coexist with either Choice 1 or Choice 2 as an interim measure.

    ## Bottom line

    The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned
    to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are **Choice 1 (collect
    CIP at onboarding)** or **Choice 2 (bank partner contractually assumes CIP)**,
    with **Choice 3 (de minimis)** as a business compromise. **Choice 4 (ship as-is)**
    is not recommended. The single most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay''s
    MSB status and the bank partner agreement terms, since those determine which path
    is available.

    this matter. All packets are model-only analysis based on internal facts. I must
    state this honestly and not claim authority exists.

    ## Research: legal basis for "Collect CIP at onboarding"

    ### Research scope and support status

    I reviewed all four research packets in this matter (the state licensing, OFAC/AML,
    USDC, and $3,000-threshold runs). **No external authority was retrieved** in any
    of them — every run reports `external_authority_retrieved: false`, and the packets
    themselves state "No external authority retrieved." The one "supplied public legal
    research" source referenced in the licensing packet was flagged as low-confidence
    (0.43) with its claims stripped and explicitly did not answer the question; it
    is a supplied source, not retrieved or verified authority. So I cannot cite a
    retrieved statute, regulation, or case for the CIP-at-onboarding rule. That is
    a **research gap**, not proof that no such authority exists — the governing rules
    (31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and 31 CFR §§ 1020.220 / 1022.210) are well-established but
    were not retrieved into this matter''s record.

    ### The applicable actor and rule

    **Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts. Whether it is a
    covered financial institution (MSB/money transmitter) or an agent of a covered
    financial institution is the controlling unknown — it determines which CIP rule
    applies and whether the obligation falls on Mosaic Relay at all.

    **Rule (as analyzed, not retrieved):** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and its implementing
    regulations, a covered financial institution must maintain a written Customer
    Identification Program that collects the customer''s name, date of birth (for
    individuals), address, and identification number **before or at the time of opening
    the account** — not after a transaction threshold is crossed. For money services
    businesses, the MSB CIP rule (31 CFR § 1022.210) applies; for banks, 31 CFR §
    1020.220. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when the customer
    first uses the service to send or receive funds, so the first payout to an unverified
    seller is the violation point.

    ### Supporting and contrary authority

    - **Supporting (analyzed, not retrieved):** The CIP-timing rule supports collecting
    identity at onboarding. The prior research packet on the $3,000 threshold (the
    internal record) reaches this conclusion, but it is **model-only** — it cites
    no retrieved authority and explicitly flags that the legal conclusions "should
    be verified against current FinCEN regulations and guidance."

    - **Contrary (analyzed, not retrieved):** The only candidate contrary position
    is that Mosaic Relay is a pure technology provider with no control over funds,
    in which case the CIP obligation may fall entirely on the bank partner. This is
    a **factual** alternative, not a legal exception, and it is unconfirmed. No retrieved
    authority supports either side.

    ### Distinguishing business risk reduction from legal permission

    This is the critical distinction. **Collecting CIP at onboarding is a risk-reduction
    measure, not a grant of legal permission.** It does not create a right to operate;
    it reduces the compliance exposure that arises from the current deferred-identity
    design. The legal question of *whether Mosaic Relay may initiate payouts at all*
    (its money transmission licensing posture) is separate and unresolved. Collecting
    CIP at onboarding reduces the CIP/AML violation but does not resolve the licensing,
    OFAC-timing, USDC, or beneficial-ownership issues. So the choice is best understood
    as: **it removes one identified violation, but it does not by itself make the
    product lawful to ship.**

    ### Material facts and exceptions

    **Known facts:** Sellers onboard with email + bank account only; identity deferred
    to $3,000; Mosaic Relay initiates ACH payouts; operates as agent under bank partner''s
    license; bank partner has seen the deck.

    **Unknown facts:** Whether Mosaic Relay is an MSB registered with FinCEN; whether
    it holds state money transmitter licenses; whether the bank partner has contractually
    accepted CIP; whether Mosaic Relay controls funds.

    **Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The $3,000 Travel
    Rule recordkeeping threshold (31 CFR § 1020.410(a)) is a separate per-transaction
    obligation and creates no CIP exemption. Risk-based flexibility exists for *how*
    verification is completed, not for *whether* identity is collected before the
    relationship is established.

    ### Reassessment of the selected path and connected paths

    The research does not change the recommendation that **collecting CIP at onboarding
    is the compliant path** for the CIP-timing issue — but it sharpens the caveat
    that this rests on **unverified, model-only analysis** and on the **unconfirmed
    premise** that Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution. The path is conditional
    on that premise. If Mosaic Relay is a pure technology provider, the obligation
    may shift to the bank (the "rely on bank CIP" path), and the two paths can coexist.
    The research also confirms the path does **not** resolve the connected licensing,
    OFAC-timing, USDC, and beneficial-ownership issues — those remain open regardless.

    **Bottom line:** The "Collect CIP at onboarding" choice is the right direction
    for the CIP-timing issue, but its legal basis is **not yet supported by retrieved
    authority** in this matter''s record. Before relying on it as the compliance foundation,
    counsel should obtain and verify the actual FinCEN CIP regulations and confirm
    Mosaic Relay''s MSB/licensing status. This is a research gap to close, not a confirmed
    legal conclusion.

# Matter chat

## You · 2026-09-04T03:42:57+00:00

From: Priya, PM — Marketplace Payments
Subject: Instant Payouts — can we ship Q1?

We're launching Instant Payouts for marketplace sellers. Plan:

Sellers onboard with email + bank account only. We collect full identity info once a seller crosses $3,000 in cumulative payouts — engineering picked that because it's "the BSA threshold."
Payouts go out via our bank partner's ACH rails. We're the ones initiating.
International sellers can elect payout in USDC through Circuit (a licensed exchange we contract with); the seller gives us a wallet address, which may be an exchange account or their own wallet.
OFAC screening runs nightly in batch against the day's payouts. Real-time screening adds ~400ms and tanks the "instant" promise.
Business sellers upload a W-9 and we take the signer's name. We're not asking for owners — the UI is already 11 screens.
We show sellers a progress bar: "$1,240 of $3,000 until full verification."

Bank partner has seen the deck. Can we ship Q1?

## Themis.ai · 2026-09-04T03:43:17+00:00

Here's my orientation to the request.
## Summary of the request
Priya (PM, Marketplace Payments) is asking whether Mosaic Relay can ship **Instant Payouts for marketplace sellers in Q1**. The proposed design:
- Sellers onboard with **email + bank account only**; full identity is deferred until **$3,000 in cumulative payouts** (engineering picked this as "the BSA threshold").
- Payouts go out via the **bank partner's ACH rails**, with Mosaic Relay initiating.
- **International sellers** can elect payout in **USDC via Circuit** (a licensed exchange), to a wallet address that may be an exchange account or the seller's own wallet.
- **OFAC screening runs nightly in batch** against the day's payouts (real-time screening would add ~400ms and break "instant").
- **Business sellers** upload a W-9 and we take the signer's name — **no owners collected** (UI already 11 screens).
- Sellers see a progress bar: **"$1,240 of $3,000 until full verification."**
The bank partner has seen the deck. **Can we ship Q1?**
## My read before we go further
This design has several serious compliance red flags that will likely block a Q1 ship as proposed, regardless of the bank partner's comfort:
1. **The $3,000 threshold is not a real BSA threshold.** There is no $3,000 BSA customer-identification trigger. The $3,000 figure is associated with money-transmission registration thresholds and cash-transaction reporting (CTR), not with deferring identity collection. Under BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP **before** establishing the account relationship — not after a payout threshold. If Mosaic Relay is a money transmitter, deferring identity until $3,000 is likely non-compliant. This is the foundation of the whole onboarding design, so it's my top question.
2. **OFAC screening after payouts go out** means funds are released before screening — a sanctions compliance problem.
3. **Initiating ACH payouts** raises whether Mosaic Relay needs its own money transmission licenses.
4. **USDC payouts to international sellers** (including self-hosted wallets) raise distinct sanctions/AML/licensing questions.
5. **No beneficial-owner collection** for business sellers is a KYB/AML gap.
6. **Publicly showing the $3,000 threshold** may facilitate evasion.
I've recorded the reported facts and issues. Here are my prioritized questions — the first is the most decision-changing:
**Q1 — What is the legal basis for the $3,000 cumulative payout threshold before collecting full identity?**
- It was purely an engineering/product choice, no legal basis confirmed
- It's tied to a money transmission licensing threshold we believe applies
- We believe it's a BSA/CIP customer identification requirement
- It's a requirement our bank partner imposed
- Not sure — need legal to determine the right threshold
**Q2 — What is Mosaic Relay's money transmission licensing posture for initiating these ACH payouts?**
- Mosaic Relay holds its own money transmission licenses
- We operate as an agent/under the bank partner's license
- We hold no money transmission licenses
- Not sure — need to confirm with compliance
**Q3 — Which jurisdictions are the international sellers located in for the USDC payout option?** (multi-select: UK, EU, Canada, Latin America, Asia, countries with sanctions exposure, not yet determined)
**Q4 — Is the nightly batch OFAC screening acceptable, or must screening occur before payouts are released?**
- Screening must happen before funds are released
- Nightly batch after payout is acceptable to us/our bank
- Not sure — need legal to advise on timing
Answer these (step by step or all together) and I'll refine the assessment.

## Themis.ai · 2026-09-04T05:18:21+00:00

I've finalized the intake and set up the workstreams. Here's the summary.
## Assessment complete — Q1 as designed is not shippable
The design has multiple compliance blockers that a bank partner's deck review does not cure. The three highest-priority blockers, in order of how much they move the decision:
1. **Deferred identity collection ($3,000 threshold) — blocker.** You confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice with no legal basis. There is no $3,000 BSA customer-identification threshold; a money transmitter must collect CIP **before** establishing the account relationship, not after a payout threshold. This is the foundation of the onboarding and must be redesigned.
2. **Money transmission licensing — unresolved.** Operating as an agent under the bank partner's license does not resolve Mosaic Relay's own posture for **initiating** ACH payouts, nor its own CIP/AML and sanctions obligations. Needs a determination before ship.
3. **OFAC screening after payouts go out — blocker.** Nightly batch screening releases funds before screening completes, risking release to sanctioned parties. Screening must occur before funds are released.
Plus two high-risk items needing work: **USDC payouts to Mexico** (sanctions/AML/virtual-asset questions, especially to self-hosted wallets) and **no beneficial-owner collection** for business sellers — and a medium-risk item on the **public $3,000 progress bar** (evasion/disclosure risk).
## What I've set up
- **Intake marked complete** with the full fact, issue, and assumption record saved.
- **Six work items** created, prioritized: (1) redesign identity collection at onboarding, (2) determine money transmission licensing posture, (3) move OFAC screening before payout release, (4) design sanctions/AML controls for USDC payouts to Mexico, (5) assess beneficial-owner collection, (6) review the public progress bar.
## What would change this
- **Working assumption:** Operating as an agent under the bank partner's license does not by itself resolve Mosaic Relay's own CIP/AML and sanctions obligations. If the bank partner's license and CIP program fully cover Mosaic Relay's onboarding, the deferred-identity and licensing concerns weaken materially — but the OFAC-before-release and USDC issues would remain.
- **Working assumption:** The OFAC timing is effectively nightly batch after payouts are released, per the design. If screening actually runs before release, that blocker drops.
- **Not examined:** The specific money transmission licensing requirements in the states where Mosaic Relay operates, and Mexico's virtual-asset/sanctions treatment of USDC payouts to self-hosted wallets — neither was researched in the vault. These are the two highest-value research items to resolve next.
Want me to move this matter into research and start on the licensing and OFAC workstreams?

## You · 2026-09-04T06:15:57+00:00

Can you go into beneficial-owner for me

## Themis.ai · 2026-09-04T06:16:15+00:00

I have the relevant context. Here's the deep dive on beneficial-owner.
## Beneficial-owner — what's at stake in this design
The proposed flow takes the **W-9 signer's name only** for business sellers and collects **no owners**. That's the gap. Here's the analysis.
### The core problem: the W-9 signer is not the beneficial owner
A W-9 captures the entity's tax identification and the person signing the form — typically an authorized representative. It does **not** identify who actually owns or controls the entity. So "we take the signer's name" gives you a name that may have nothing to do with the person whose identity matters for AML/sanctions purposes. That's the fundamental flaw.
### What the law requires (US)
**FinCEN CDD Rule (31 CFR 1010.230)** — if Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution (which it likely is as a money transmitter/MSB), it must identify and verify beneficial owners of legal entity customers **at account opening**:
- **Ownership prong:** any individual owning 25% or more of the entity's equity.
- **Control prong:** a single individual with significant responsibility to control/manage/direct the entity (CEO, CFO, managing member, etc.).
- Requires collecting name, DOB, address, and ID number, and verifying identity.
**Corporate Transparency Act (CTA, 31 USC 5336)** — a separate, entity-level reporting obligation to FinCEN for BOI. Note: the CTA's reporting requirement has been subject to litigation and injunctions; its status has shifted over time, so treat the CTA piece as needing current verification rather than settled.
### The key tension with this design
The CDD Rule applies at **account opening** — which here means when Mosaic Relay enables payout services for a seller. The design defers *all* identity to a $3,000 threshold, and even then only collects the signer's name, not owners. So the beneficial-owner gap is not just a "nice to have" — it's the same deferred-identity problem as the $3,000 threshold, applied to business entities. **Collecting the signer's name at $3,000 does not satisfy the ownership/control prongs.**
### The "UI is already 11 screens" argument doesn't carry
The design rationale is that adding owner collection is too much UI. But the CDD Rule doesn't have a UI-cost exemption. The practical answer is a **risk-based approach**: collect beneficial-owner information at onboarding (name, DOB, address, ID), run automated database/sanctions checks, and enable limited activity while completing enhanced verification within a short window — rather than collecting nothing and deferring everything to $3,000.
### What would change this
- **Working assumption:** Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution subject to the CDD Rule. If it's structured as a pure technology provider with no money transmission (which the licensing question hasn't resolved), the CDD Rule may not apply directly — but the sanctions and reputational risk would still demand owner collection, and the bank partner's program may impose it anyway.
- **Open fork:** Whether Mosaic Relay can rely on the marketplace to collect/verify owners (delegated model) or must do it directly. If delegated, Mosaic Relay stays legally responsible and needs strong contractual indemnities and audit rights.
- **Not examined:** The specific terms of Mosaic Relay's contracts with its acquiring bank/processor, which may impose owner/control-person disclosure requirements beyond the CDD Rule. Those contracts weren't provided. Also, the CTA's current enforceability status needs verification given the litigation history.
**Bottom line:** The beneficial-owner gap is a real blocker on its own, independent of the $3,000 threshold. The fix is to collect owner information at onboarding (risk-based, with automated checks), not to add it at the $3,000 point or rely on the W-9 signer's name.
Want me to spin this into a dedicated work item with a concrete risk-based owner-collection design, or fold it into the existing identity-redesign workstream?

## Themis.ai · 2026-09-05T14:45:27+00:00

## Why the jurisdiction question matters
This question drives the **USDC payout leg** — the part of the design that is most distinct from the fiat ACH flow and carries the highest regulatory uncertainty. The answer changes the analysis in three ways:
1. **Sanctions exposure.** Whether any seller is in an OFAC-sanctioned or high-risk jurisdiction determines whether the nightly-batch screening issue is a theoretical risk or an active one. A seller in a sanctioned jurisdiction would require immediate blocking, not deferred screening.
2. **Virtual-asset money transmission licensing.** Whether Mosaic Relay needs a license or registration to pay out USDC depends on the local law of the seller's jurisdiction. This is separate from the US state money transmission analysis.
3. **AML/KYB expectations.** Some jurisdictions impose their own customer-identification and virtual-asset service provider (VASP) registration rules that would apply even if the US analysis were clean.
## What you've told me so far
There's a conflict in the record. Earlier you answered **"Asia"**, then later answered **"Mexico"** for the same question. Those are materially different answers, and the record now holds both. This matters because:
- **Mexico** has a specific regime — the Fintech Law (Ley Fintech) requires VASP registration with the CNBV, and USDC payouts to Mexican residents may trigger local licensing/registration obligations.
- **Asia** is not a single jurisdiction — it spans countries with very different virtual-asset regimes (e.g., Singapore, Japan, Hong Kong, India, China), some of which ban or heavily restrict crypto payouts.
## Likely conditional answer
**If the sellers are in Mexico:** The USDC payout leg likely triggers Mexican VASP registration obligations under the Fintech Law, and Mosaic Relay's agent-under-bank-partner posture almost certainly does not cover virtual-asset activity in Mexico. This makes the USDC option a **separate compliance project** that is unlikely to be shippable in Q1 without local counsel and registration.
**If the sellers are in Asia:** The answer depends entirely on *which* Asian country. In permissive regimes (Singapore, Japan) the flow may be workable with the right licensing; in restrictive ones (China, India) it may be effectively unavailable. Either way, it's a jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction analysis, not a single answer.
## Short alternative
If the jurisdiction is **not yet determined** or the seller base is small/limited, the cleanest path is to **defer the USDC option entirely** and ship fiat ACH payouts in Q1, adding USDC once the jurisdiction-specific licensing and sanctions work is done. That preserves the Q1 timeline for the core product while removing the highest-uncertainty feature.
## What would change this
- **Working assumption:** The USDC payout leg carries its own licensing and sanctions obligations that the bank-partner agent relationship does not cover. If Circuit (the licensed exchange) assumes the compliance burden for the USDC transfers, some risk shifts — but Mosaic Relay's role in initiating the payout still creates exposure.
- **Open fork:** Whether Mexico requires CNBV registration for these payouts. If yes, the USDC option needs a separate workstream; if no, it may be more feasible.
- **Not examined:** The specific terms of Mosaic Relay's contract with Circuit, which may allocate compliance responsibilities, and whether any sellers sit in sanctioned jurisdictions.
**Bottom line:** The jurisdiction answer is material because it determines whether the USDC leg is a manageable feature or a separate compliance project that can't ship in Q1. Given the conflict between "Asia" and "Mexico" in the record, the single most useful next step is to **confirm which it actually is** — that one fact changes the whole USDC analysis. Want me to flag that conflict as a work item so it gets resolved?

## Themis.ai · 2026-09-06T14:57:26+00:00

## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold
**The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until $3,000 is likely non-compliant.
**Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design. If the threshold is legally unsupportable, then every seller who receives payouts below $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money laundering, sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you've already confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice with no legal basis. So the design rests on a misconception, and the question is what to do about it.
**One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000 recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening, not after a dollar threshold.
## The test
**Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial institution.
**Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).
**The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210 for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement a written CIP that collects the customer's name, DOB (individuals), address, and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout to an unverified seller is the violation point.
**Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule's $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a different obligation and does not create a CIP exemption. Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed (e.g., phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship is established.
## Unknown facts that change the analysis
- **Mosaic Relay's MSB registration status.** If it is not a covered financial institution and is purely a technology provider with no control over funds, the CIP obligation may fall entirely on the bank partner. But the facts state Mosaic Relay is "the one initiating" ACH payouts, which points toward its own obligations.
- **Bank partner's written agreement.** If the bank has contractually accepted full CIP responsibility (including identity collection through Mosaic Relay's onboarding), the analysis shifts. The bank's deck review does **not** constitute this acceptance.
- **Whether any BSA exemption applies.** None are known to apply to CIP timing here.
## Conditional routes
**Path A — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).** Redesign onboarding to collect full identity before any payout is released. This is the compliant approach. The UI-friction concern can be addressed through progressive disclosure, third-party identity verification APIs, or risk-based simplified onboarding for low-risk sellers — but identity must be collected before the first payout, not after $3,000.
**Path B — Rely on the bank partner's CIP.** If the bank agrees in writing to take full responsibility for CIP (including collecting identity through Mosaic Relay's flow), Mosaic Relay may be able to rely on the bank's program. This requires the bank to explicitly accept the responsibility and for Mosaic Relay's onboarding to meet the bank's CIP standards. The deck review does not satisfy this.
**Path C — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).** The CIP violation is a core AML requirement, not a technicality. Operating with unidentified customers receiving payouts creates enforcement risk, civil money penalties, and reputational harm.
## Business alternatives
- **Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount** (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicly display the threshold. This is a risk-based approach some regulators may tolerate for truly de minimis amounts, but it is not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules and carries residual risk. Requires a documented risk assessment and legal opinion.
- **Progressive/risk-based onboarding** — collect basic identity at signup, complete enhanced verification within a short window while limiting activity. This preserves some of the "instant" promise while closing the unidentified-customer window.
**Bottom line:** The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are Path A (collect CIP at onboarding) or Path B (bank partner contractually assumes CIP), with a de minimis or risk-based variant as a business compromise. The single most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay's MSB status and the bank partner agreement terms, since those determine which path is available.

## Themis.ai · 2026-09-06T18:16:50+00:00

## The issue: the public $3,000 verification progress bar
**The saved issue:** Publicly displaying the $3,000 verification threshold via a progress bar may facilitate evasion and raises funds-flow/consumer disclosure concerns.
**Why the fact matters:** The progress bar tells every seller — including bad actors — the exact dollar amount they can receive before identity verification kicks in. That's an open invitation to structure payouts just below the threshold to stay unidentified. It also creates a misleading impression that $3,000 is a legally meaningful compliance line, when you've already confirmed it has no legal basis.
**One short contrast:** A progress bar that says "$1,240 of $3,000 until full verification" is functionally an instruction manual for evasion — a bad actor keeps payouts under $3,000 and never gets identified. Compare that to a design with no public threshold, where a bad actor can't tell where the verification line is and can't reliably structure around it.
## The applicable test
**Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating payouts and designing the seller-facing onboarding.
**Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML) plus general consumer-protection/funds-flow disclosure principles.
**The test:** There is no single statute that says "don't display a verification threshold." The concern is twofold:
1. **AML/structuring:** Under BSA/AML, a financial institution's AML program must be reasonably designed to detect and prevent money laundering and structuring. Publicly advertising the exact threshold at which verification triggers is an AML program weakness — it facilitates structuring (deliberately keeping transactions below a reporting/verification threshold to avoid detection). This is a risk-based program deficiency, not a bright-line rule.
2. **Consumer disclosure/funds-flow:** Displaying a "$X of $3,000 until full verification" bar can be misleading if it implies $3,000 is a legal or regulatory threshold, or if it misrepresents the funds-flow/verification process. This raises accuracy and fair-dealing concerns.
**Exceptions:** None material. The concern applies regardless of whether the underlying $3,000 threshold is itself compliant — the display compounds the problem either way.
## Known vs. unknown facts
**Known:**
- The progress bar reads "$1,240 of $3,000 until full verification" (reported).
- The $3,000 threshold has no legal basis — it was purely an engineering/product choice (confirmed).
- Identity is deferred until $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).
**Unknown:**
- Whether the bar is shown to all sellers or only some (e.g., only those approaching the threshold).
- Whether the bar is the only place the threshold is disclosed, or whether it appears in terms/onboarding docs.
- Whether any consumer-protection or state disclosure rules apply to Mosaic Relay's specific payout flow (not researched in the vault).
## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take
**Choice 1 — Remove the progress bar entirely.**
- **Depends on:** Nothing material; this is a product/UI decision.
- **Next consequence:** Sellers no longer see the threshold; the structuring cue is removed.
- **Risks:** Minimal. Some sellers may be confused about verification status, but that's a UX concern, not a compliance one.
- **Trade-off:** Loses a transparency feature, but removes the evasion cue and the misleading "legal threshold" implication.
- **Remaining work:** Product change to remove the bar; confirm no other surface discloses the threshold.
**Choice 2 — Keep the bar but reframe it as a status indicator, not a threshold.**
- **Depends on:** Whether the underlying deferred-identity design is itself changed. If identity moves to onboarding (the recommended fix for the $3,000 issue), the bar becomes moot — there's no threshold to display.
- **Next consequence:** If the $3,000 threshold is eliminated, the bar either disappears or becomes a generic "verification in progress" indicator.
- **Risks:** If the threshold stays and the bar is merely reworded, the structuring cue remains.
- **Trade-off:** Preserves some transparency while removing the explicit dollar threshold.
- **Remaining work:** Coordinate with the identity-redesign workstream; reword the bar to avoid stating a dollar threshold.
**Choice 3 — Keep the bar as-is.**
- **Depends on:** Accepting the structuring and disclosure risk.
- **Next consequence:** Bad actors can structure below $3,000 to stay unidentified; the bar misrepresents $3,000 as a legal line.
- **Risks:** AML program weakness (structuring facilitation), potential consumer-disclosure issue, and it compounds the already-non-compliant deferred-identity design.
- **Trade-off:** No product change, but carries the highest risk.
- **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.
**Choice 4 — Fix the underlying threshold first, then the bar resolves itself.**
- **Depends on:** The identity-redesign workstream (collect CIP at onboarding). If identity is collected at onboarding, there is no $3,000 threshold and no bar to display.
- **Next consequence:** The progress bar issue disappears as a side effect of the larger fix.
- **Risks:** Depends on the timeline of the identity redesign; if that slips, the bar remains a live issue.
- **Trade-off:** This is the cleanest resolution but is gated on the bigger onboarding redesign.
- **Remaining work:** The identity-redesign workstream; then remove or repurpose the bar.
## Bottom line
The progress bar is a **secondary issue that compounds the primary $3,000 problem** — it advertises the exact amount a bad actor can receive before verification. The cleanest resolution is **Choice 4**: fix the underlying deferred-identity design (collect CIP at onboarding), which makes the bar moot. If the threshold must remain for now, **Choice 1 (remove the bar)** is the lowest-risk interim step, because it removes the structuring cue and the misleading "legal threshold" implication without waiting on the larger redesign. **Choice 3 (keep as-is)** is not recommended.

- Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner's license (reported).
- The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).
**Unknown:**
- Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN and holds state money transmitter licenses (not confirmed).
- Whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP responsibility in the agency agreement (not reviewed).
- Whether any BSA exemption applies (none known, but not affirmatively ruled out).
## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take
**Choice 1 — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).**
- **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being a covered financial institution (or the bank not assuming CIP), so Mosaic Relay must collect identity before first payout.
- **Next consequence:** Compliant onboarding; identity collected before any payout is released; the unidentified-customer window closes.
- **Risks:** UI friction and engineering effort. This is an expected cost, not a legal risk.
- **Trade-off:** More onboarding friction vs. closing the core AML violation.
- **Remaining work:** Redesign onboarding to collect CIP before first payout; confirm MSB registration and state licensing posture.
**Choice 2 — Rely on the bank partner's CIP.**
- **Depends on:** The bank partner contractually accepting full CIP responsibility through Mosaic Relay's onboarding flow.
- **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may rely on the bank's CIP program; onboarding must meet the bank's CIP standards.
- **Risks:** If the bank does not explicitly accept responsibility in writing, the obligation remains with Mosaic Relay. The deck review does not constitute acceptance.
- **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees and Mosaic Relay's flow meets bank standards.
- **Remaining work:** Obtain written CIP acceptance from the bank; align onboarding to bank CIP standards.
**Choice 3 — Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount.**
- **Depends on:** Business insisting on some pre-verification payouts; limit to a small amount (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicize the threshold.
- **Next consequence:** Reduced unidentified-customer exposure, but residual risk remains.
- **Risks:** Not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules; carries residual enforcement risk. This is a possible risk, not an expected consequence.
- **Trade-off:** Preserves some instant-payout value vs. residual compliance risk.
- **Remaining work:** Document a risk assessment; obtain a legal opinion on the de minimis approach.
**Choice 4 — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).**
- **Depends on:** No change to the deferred-identity design.
- **Next consequence:** Ships with unidentified customers receiving payouts — a core AML violation.
- **Risks:** Enforcement risk, civil money penalties, reputational harm. These are expected consequences of a core AML violation, not merely possible risks.
- **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest compliance exposure.
- **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.
## Bottom line
The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are **Choice 1 (collect CIP at onboarding)** or **Choice 2 (bank partner contractually assumes CIP)**, with **Choice 3 (de minimis)** as a business compromise. **Choice 4 (ship as-is)** is not recommended. The single most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay's MSB status and the bank partner agreement terms, since those determine which path is available.

## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold
**The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until $3,000 is likely non-compliant.
**Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design. If the threshold is legally unsupportable, every seller who receives payouts below $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money laundering, sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you've confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.
**One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000 recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening, not after a dollar threshold.
## The applicable test
**Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial institution.
**Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).
**The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210 for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement a written CIP that collects the customer's name, DOB (individuals), address, and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout to an unverified seller is the violation point.
**Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule's $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a separate obligation and creates no CIP exemption. Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed (e.g., phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship is established.
## Known vs. unknown facts
**Known:**
- Sellers onboard with email and bank account only; full identity is deferred to $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).

**Unknown:**

- Whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP responsibility in the agency agreement (not reviewed).
- Whether any BSA exemption applies (none known, but not affirmatively ruled out).

- **Remaining work:** Redesign onboarding to collect CIP before first payout; confirm MSB registration and state licensing posture.
**Choice 2 — Rely on the bank partner's CIP.**
- **Depends on:** The bank partner contractually accepting full CIP responsibility through Mosaic Relay's onboarding flow.
- **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may rely on the bank's CIP program; onboarding must meet the bank's CIP standards.
- **Risks:** If the bank does not explicitly accept responsibility in writing, the obligation remains with Mosaic Relay. The deck review does not constitute acceptance.
- **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees and Mosaic Relay's flow meets bank standards.
- **Remaining work:** Obtain written CIP acceptance from the bank; align onboarding to bank CIP standards.
**Choice 3 — Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount.**
- **Depends on:** Business insisting on some pre-verification payouts; limit to a small amount (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicize the threshold.
- **Next consequence:** Reduced unidentified-customer exposure, but residual risk remains.
- **Risks:** Not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules; carries residual enforcement risk. This is a possible risk, not an expected consequence.
- **Trade-off:** Preserves some instant-payout value vs. residual compliance risk.

**Choice 4 — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).**
- **Depends on:** No change to the deferred-identity design.
- **Next consequence:** Ships with unidentified customers receiving payouts — a core AML violation.

- **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest compliance exposure.

The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned to collect identity at onboarding. ## Themis.ai · 2026-09-07T00:34:59+00:00

## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold
**The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until $3,000 is likely non-compliant.
**Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design. If the threshold is legally unsupportable, every seller who receives payouts below $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money laundering, sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you've confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.
**One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000 recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening, not after a dollar threshold.
## The applicable test
**Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial institution.
**Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).
**The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210 for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement a written CIP that collects the customer's name, DOB (individuals), address, and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout to an unverified seller is the violation point.
**Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule's $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a separate obligation and creates no CIP exemption. Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed (e.g., phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship is established.
## Known vs. unknown facts
**Known:**
- Sellers onboard with email and bank account only; full identity is deferred to $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).
- The $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice, no legal basis confirmed (reported).
- Mosaic Relay initiates the ACH payouts (reported).
- Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner's license (reported).
- The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).
**Unknown:**
- Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN and holds state money transmitter licenses (not confirmed).
- Whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP responsibility in the agency agreement (not reviewed).
- Whether any BSA exemption applies (none known, but not affirmatively ruled out).
## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take
**Choice 1 — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).**
- **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being a covered financial institution (or the bank not assuming CIP), so Mosaic Relay must collect identity before first payout.
- **Next consequence:** Compliant onboarding; identity collected before any payout is released; the unidentified-customer window closes.
- **Risks:** UI friction and engineering effort. This is an expected cost, not a legal risk.
- **Trade-off:** More onboarding friction vs. closing the core AML violation.
- **Remaining work:** Redesign onboarding to collect CIP before first payout; confirm MSB registration and state licensing posture.
**Choice 2 — Rely on the bank partner's CIP.**
- **Depends on:** The bank partner contractually accepting full CIP responsibility through Mosaic Relay's onboarding flow.
- **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may rely on the bank's CIP program; onboarding must meet the bank's CIP standards.
- **Risks:** If the bank does not explicitly accept responsibility in writing, the obligation remains with Mosaic Relay. The deck review does not constitute acceptance.
- **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees and Mosaic Relay's flow meets bank standards.
- **Remaining work:** Obtain written CIP acceptance from the bank; align onboarding to bank CIP standards.
**Choice 3 — Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount.**
- **Depends on:** Business insisting on some pre-verification payouts; limit to a small amount (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicize the threshold.
- **Next consequence:** Reduced unidentified-customer exposure, but residual risk remains.
- **Risks:** Not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules; carries residual enforcement risk. This is a possible risk, not an expected consequence.
- **Trade-off:** Preserves some instant-payout value vs. residual compliance risk.
- **Remaining work:** Document a risk assessment; obtain a legal opinion on the de minimis approach.
**Choice 4 — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).**
- **Depends on:** No change to the deferred-identity design.
- **Next consequence:** Ships with unidentified customers receiving payouts — a core AML violation.
- **Risks:** Enforcement risk, civil money penalties, reputational harm. These are expected consequences of a core AML violation, not merely possible risks.
- **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest compliance exposure.
- **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.
## How the choices relate
The key distinction is between **agreeing to a redesign** and **implementing it**. Choosing to redesign (Choice 1) does not instantly replace the current design — until the CIP-at-onboarding flow is actually built and deployed, the existing deferred-identity route remains the operating state. So Choice 1 and Choice 4 can coexist in time: the business can commit to the redesign while the current design continues to operate during the build window. Choice 1 only *replaces* Choice 4 once the redesign is implemented. Similarly, Choice 2 (bank CIP) and Choice 1 are not mutually exclusive — the bank could assume CIP while Mosaic Relay still collects identity at onboarding; they can coexist. Choice 3 (de minimis) is a variant that can coexist with either Choice 1 or Choice 2 as an interim measure.
## Connections to the other saved issues
This issue is the **root** of the matter's compliance problems, and it connects to every other saved issue:
- **Licensing posture (ISS-...46f961836e842b02):** Whether Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution determines whether the CIP obligation falls on it at all. This issue **depends on** the licensing issue — if Mosaic Relay is a pure technology provider with no control over funds, the CIP obligation may shift to the bank, changing the whole analysis. Conversely, resolving the licensing posture may resolve which CIP path is available.
- **OFAC screening timing (ISS-...0ca150debaa4249f):** Both issues share the same underlying condition — Mosaic Relay's status as a covered financial institution. The deferred-identity window and the post-release screening window are **shared_condition** problems: both leave unidentified/unscreened parties receiving funds. Fixing identity collection at onboarding narrows but does not close the OFAC timing issue.
- **USDC payouts (ISS-...980c2188f70b5315):** The deferred-identity gap **compounds** the USDC risk — an unidentified seller receiving USDC to a self-hosted wallet is the highest-risk combination. Collecting CIP at onboarding reduces but does not resolve the separate virtual-asset licensing and sanctions questions.
- **Beneficial ownership (ISS-...22fd89b609272861):** Both are **shared_condition** KYB/AML gaps — neither collects the identity information BSA/AML requires. Fixing CIP timing for individuals does not fix the separate beneficial-ownership gap for business sellers; they are distinct obligations that must each be addressed.
- **Progress bar (ISS-...704b6c4c5d335bfe):** The progress bar is a direct **consequence** of the $3,000 threshold — it advertises the exact amount a seller can receive before verification. If this issue is fixed by collecting CIP at onboarding, the threshold disappears and the progress bar becomes moot (**may_resolve**). Until the redesign is implemented, the bar remains a live issue.
## Bottom line
The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are **Choice 1 (collect CIP at onboarding)** or **Choice 2 (bank partner contractually assumes CIP)**, with **Choice 3 (de minimis)** as a business compromise. **Choice 4 (ship as-is)** is not recommended. The single most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay's MSB status and the bank partner agreement terms, since those determine which path is available — and that same confirmation feeds the licensing, OFAC, and USDC issues.

## Themis.ai · 2026-09-07T00:40:38+00:00

## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold
**The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until $3,000 is likely non-compliant.
**Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design. If the threshold is legally unsupportable, every seller who receives payouts below $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money laundering, sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you've confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.
**One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000 recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening, not after a dollar threshold.
## The applicable test
**Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial institution.
**Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).
**The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210 for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement a written CIP that collects the customer's name, DOB (individuals), address, and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout to an unverified seller is the violation point.
**Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule's $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a separate obligation and creates no CIP exemption. Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed (e.g., phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship is established.
## Known vs. unknown facts
**Known:**
- Sellers onboard with email and bank account only; full identity is deferred to $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).
- The $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice, no legal basis confirmed (reported).
- Mosaic Relay initiates the ACH payouts (reported).
- Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner's license (reported).
- The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).
**Unknown:**
- Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN and holds state money transmitter licenses (not confirmed).
- Whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP responsibility in the agency agreement (not reviewed).
- Whether any BSA exemption applies (none known, but not affirmatively ruled out).
## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take
**Choice 1 — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).**
- **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being a covered financial institution (or the bank not assuming CIP), so Mosaic Relay must collect identity before first payout.
- **Next consequence:** Compliant onboarding; identity collected before any payout is released; the unidentified-customer window closes.
- **Risks:** UI friction and engineering effort. This is an expected cost, not a legal risk.
- **Trade-off:** More onboarding friction vs. closing the core AML violation.
- **Remaining work:** Redesign onboarding to collect CIP before first payout; confirm MSB registration and state licensing posture.
**Choice 2 — Rely on the bank partner's CIP.**
- **Depends on:** The bank partner contractually accepting full CIP responsibility through Mosaic Relay's onboarding flow.
- **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may rely on the bank's CIP program; onboarding must meet the bank's CIP standards.
- **Risks:** If the bank does not explicitly accept responsibility in writing, the obligation remains with Mosaic Relay. The deck review does not constitute acceptance.
- **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees and Mosaic Relay's flow meets bank standards.
- **Remaining work:** Obtain written CIP acceptance from the bank; align onboarding to bank CIP standards.
**Choice 3 — Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount.**
- **Depends on:** Business insisting on some pre-verification payouts; limit to a small amount (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicize the threshold.
- **Next consequence:** Reduced unidentified-customer exposure, but residual risk remains.
- **Risks:** Not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules; carries residual enforcement risk. This is a possible risk, not an expected consequence.
- **Trade-off:** Preserves some instant-payout value vs. residual compliance risk.
- **Remaining work:** Document a risk assessment; obtain a legal opinion on the de minimis approach.
**Choice 4 — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).**
- **Depends on:** No change to the deferred-identity design.
- **Next consequence:** Ships with unidentified customers receiving payouts — a core AML violation.
- **Risks:** Enforcement risk, civil money penalties, reputational harm. These are expected consequences of a core AML violation, not merely possible risks.
- **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest compliance exposure.
- **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.
## How the choices relate
The key distinction is between **agreeing to a redesign** and **implementing it**. Choosing to redesign (Choice 1) does not instantly replace the current design — until the CIP-at-onboarding flow is actually built and deployed, the existing deferred-identity route remains the operating state. So Choice 1 and Choice 4 can coexist in time: the business can commit to the redesign while the current design continues to operate during the build window. Choice 1 only *replaces* Choice 4 once the redesign is implemented. Similarly, Choice 2 (bank CIP) and Choice 1 are not mutually exclusive — the bank could assume CIP while Mosaic Relay still collects identity at onboarding; they can coexist. Choice 3 (de minimis) is a variant that can coexist with either Choice 1 or Choice 2 as an interim measure.
## Connections to the other saved issues
This issue is the **root** of the matter's compliance problems, and it connects to every other saved issue:
- **Licensing posture (ISS-...46f961836e842b02):** Whether Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution determines whether the CIP obligation falls on it at all. This issue **depends on** the licensing issue — if Mosaic Relay is a pure technology provider with no control over funds, the CIP obligation may shift to the bank, changing the whole analysis. Conversely, resolving the licensing posture may resolve which CIP path is available.
- **OFAC screening timing (ISS-...0ca150debaa4249f):** Both issues share the same underlying condition — Mosaic Relay's status as a covered financial institution. The deferred-identity window and the post-release screening window are **shared_condition** problems: both leave unidentified/unscreened parties receiving funds. Fixing identity collection at onboarding narrows but does not close the OFAC timing issue.
- **USDC payouts (ISS-...980c2188f70b5315):** The deferred-identity gap **compounds** the USDC risk — an unidentified seller receiving USDC to a self-hosted wallet is the highest-risk combination. Collecting CIP at onboarding reduces but does not resolve the separate virtual-asset licensing and sanctions questions.
- **Beneficial ownership (ISS-...22fd89b609272861):** Both are **shared_condition** KYB/AML gaps — neither collects the identity information BSA/AML requires. Fixing CIP timing for individuals does not fix the separate beneficial-ownership gap for business sellers; they are distinct obligations that must each be addressed.
- **Progress bar (ISS-...704b6c4c5d335bfe):** The progress bar is a direct **consequence** of the $3,000 threshold — it advertises the exact amount a seller can receive before verification. If this issue is fixed by collecting CIP at onboarding, the threshold disappears and the progress bar becomes moot (**may_resolve**). Until the redesign is implemented, the bar remains a live issue.
## Bottom line
The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are **Choice 1 (collect CIP at onboarding)** or **Choice 2 (bank partner contractually assumes CIP)**, with **Choice 3 (de minimis)** as a business compromise. **Choice 4 (ship as-is)** is not recommended. The single most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay's MSB status and the bank partner agreement terms, since those determine which path is available — and that same confirmation feeds the licensing, OFAC, and USDC issues.
