## Themis.ai · 2026-09-07T01:17:24+00:00

The target issue for this run is **ISS-7eb9a27f11-46f961836e842b02** — Mosaic Relay's money transmission licensing posture. Here's the path analysis.
## The issue: money transmission licensing posture
**The saved issue:** Operating as an agent under the bank partner's license does not resolve Mosaic Relay's own money transmission licensing posture for initiating ACH payouts, nor its own CIP/AML and sanctions obligations.
**Why the fact matters:** This is the threshold question that determines whether Mosaic Relay can lawfully initiate payouts at all — and it gates every other issue in the matter. If Mosaic Relay is itself a money transmitter (or is deemed to be one), it needs its own state money transmitter licenses and its own FinCEN MSB registration, and it bears its own CIP/AML and sanctions obligations. If it is a pure technology provider with no control over funds, those obligations may shift to the bank partner. The agent-under-the-bank's-license framing is not a legal determination — it's a business arrangement that must be tested against each state's money transmitter act.
**One short contrast:** "We operate as an agent under the bank partner's license" sounds like it resolves the licensing question, but it doesn't. In many states, an "agent of a licensee" exemption has specific conditions — the agent must not receive, hold, or control funds, and the principal must be responsible for the agent's acts. If Mosaic Relay is the party *initiating* the ACH entries and controlling the funds flow, it may not qualify for the exemption and may need its own license. The bank partner "seeing the deck" is not a legal determination of Mosaic Relay's own obligations.
## The applicable test
**Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts.
**Jurisdiction:** State-by-state (each state's money transmitter act) plus federal (FinCEN MSB registration).
**The test:** Money transmission licensing is a state-by-state regime. The key questions are: (1) whether initiating ACH payouts falls within the state's definition of "money transmission," and (2) whether Mosaic Relay qualifies for an exemption as an agent of a licensed bank or agent of an exempt person. The critical factual driver is whether Mosaic Relay ever **receives, holds, or controls funds** in the payout flow, or whether it acts purely as a third-party sender instructing the bank partner. If Mosaic Relay controls the funds flow, it likely needs its own licenses.
**Exceptions:** Agent-of-a-licensee exemptions exist in many states but are conditional — they typically require the agent to not hold or control funds and the principal to be responsible. Whether Mosaic Relay qualifies depends on the specific statute in each state where it operates.
## Known vs. unknown facts
**Known:**
- Mosaic Relay initiates the ACH payouts (reported).
- Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner's license (reported).
- The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).
**Unknown:**
- Whether Mosaic Relay ever receives, holds, or controls funds in the payout flow (not confirmed).
- Which specific states Mosaic Relay operates in and where sellers are located (not confirmed).
- The specific terms of the agency agreement with the bank partner (not reviewed).
- Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN (not confirmed).
- Whether the agent-of-a-licensee exemption applies in the relevant states (not confirmed).
## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take
**Choice 1 — Confirm the funds-flow and agency-agreement facts (recommended first step).**
- **Depends on:** Determining whether Mosaic Relay receives, holds, or controls funds, and reviewing the agency agreement terms.
- **Next consequence:** Establishes whether Mosaic Relay is a money transmitter needing its own licenses, or qualifies for an agent exemption.
- **Risks:** None — this is fact-gathering, not a decision.
- **Trade-off:** Requires internal and legal review before any launch decision.
- **Remaining work:** Review the agency agreement; map the funds flow; identify the states involved.
**Choice 2 — Obtain state money transmitter licenses.**
- **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being deemed a money transmitter in the relevant states (i.e., it controls funds or doesn't qualify for an exemption).
- **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay can lawfully initiate payouts in those states; it also becomes a covered financial institution with its own CIP/AML and sanctions obligations.
- **Risks:** Licensing is time-consuming and state-by-state; may not be achievable by Q1. This is an expected cost/constraint, not a legal risk.
- **Trade-off:** Full compliance vs. significant time and cost that may miss Q1.
- **Remaining work:** Identify states, prepare applications, obtain licenses, register as an MSB with FinCEN.
**Choice 3 — Restructure so the bank partner is the party initiating payouts.**
- **Depends on:** The bank partner agreeing to be the ODFI/initiating party, with Mosaic Relay as a pure technology provider that never controls funds.
- **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may qualify for an agent exemption and avoid its own licensing; the bank bears primary CIP/AML and sanctions obligations.
- **Risks:** Requires the bank to accept this role contractually; the bank may not agree. The deck review does not constitute this acceptance.
- **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees and the funds flow is restructured.
- **Remaining work:** Renegotiate the agency agreement; restructure the funds flow; obtain written confirmation from the bank.
**Choice 4 — Launch only in states where an exemption is confirmed.**
- **Depends on:** Identifying states where Mosaic Relay qualifies for an agent-of-a-licensee exemption.
- **Next consequence:** A Q1 launch is possible but only in a reduced set of states.
- **Risks:** Reduced addressable market; requires confirming each state's exemption conditions.
- **Trade-off:** Preserves some Q1 launch vs. reduced market coverage.
- **Remaining work:** State-by-state exemption analysis.
**Choice 5 — Ship as-is and accept the licensing risk (not recommended).**
- **Depends on:** No change to the current agent-under-the-bank's-license arrangement.
- **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay initiates payouts without confirming its own licensing posture.
- **Risks:** Unlicensed money transmission enforcement risk, fines, and reputational harm. This is an expected consequence if Mosaic Relay is deemed a money transmitter, not merely a possible risk.
- **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest licensing exposure.
- **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.
## How the choices relate
The key distinction is between **agreeing to a restructure** and **implementing it**. Choosing Choice 3 (restructure so the bank initiates) does not instantly change the operating state — until the agency agreement is renegotiated and the funds flow is actually restructured, the current arrangement remains. So Choice 3 and Choice 5 can coexist in time: the business can commit to the restructure while the current arrangement continues during the transition. Choice 3 only *replaces* Choice 5 once the restructure is implemented. Similarly, Choice 2 (obtain licenses) and Choice 3 (restructure) are not mutually exclusive — Mosaic Relay could obtain licenses in some states while restructuring in others; they can coexist.
## Connections to the other saved issues
This licensing issue is the **gateway** to the matter's other compliance issues, because it determines whether Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution:
- **$3,000 threshold (ISS-...8c1936ea2c58b600):** This issue **may_resolve** the threshold issue — if Mosaic Relay is a pure technology provider with no control over funds, the CIP obligation may shift to the bank, changing the whole deferred-identity analysis. Conversely, if Mosaic Relay is a money transmitter, it bears its own CIP obligation and the $3,000 threshold is non-compliant.
- **OFAC screening timing (ISS-...0ca150debaa4249f):** **shared_condition** — both depend on Mosaic Relay's status as a covered financial institution. If Mosaic Relay is a money transmitter, it bears its own sanctions obligations; if not, they may shift to the bank.
- **USDC payouts (ISS-...980c2188f70b5315):** **compounds** — the licensing question is more acute for USDC payouts, which may require separate virtual-asset money transmission licenses beyond the fiat ACH analysis. Resolving the fiat licensing posture does not resolve the USDC licensing question.
- **Beneficial ownership (ISS-...22fd89b609272861):** **shared_condition** — if Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution, the KYB/beneficial-ownership obligations apply to it directly; if not, they may fall on the bank.
- **Progress bar (ISS-...704b6c4c5d335bfe):** **depends_on** — the progress bar's consumer-disclosure concern is secondary to whether Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution at all; the licensing posture determines the framework within which the disclosure concern is assessed.
## Bottom line
The licensing posture is the **gateway issue** — it determines whether Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution and therefore whether it bears its own CIP/AML, sanctions, and KYB obligations. The single most useful next step is **Choice 1**: confirm the funds-flow facts and review the agency agreement, because that determines whether Mosaic Relay needs its own licenses (Choice 2), can restructure so the bank initiates (Choice 3), or can launch in a limited set of states (Choice 4). **Choice 5 (ship as-is)** is not recommended.

## Themis.ai · 2026-09-07T15:09:37+00:00

The target issue for this run is **ISS-7eb9a27f11-8c1936ea2c58b600** — the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold. Here's the path analysis.
## The issue: the $3,000 deferred-identity threshold
**The saved issue:** The $3,000 cumulative payout threshold is not a recognized BSA customer-identification threshold. There is no $3,000 BSA CIP trigger. Under BSA/AML, a money transmitter must collect CIP before establishing the account relationship, not after a payout threshold. Deferring identity collection until $3,000 is likely non-compliant.
**Why the fact matters:** This is the foundation of the entire onboarding design. If the threshold is legally unsupportable, every seller who receives payouts below $3,000 is an unidentified customer receiving funds — a window for money laundering, sanctions evasion, and fraud. And you've confirmed the $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice with no legal basis.
**One short contrast:** The $3,000 figure appears to be a confusion with the **$3,000 recordkeeping threshold for funds transfers** (31 CFR § 1020.410(a), the "Travel Rule") — a *per-transaction recordkeeping* obligation. That is not a *customer identification* trigger tied to *cumulative* payouts. CIP applies at account opening, not after a dollar threshold.
## The applicable test
**Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts — likely a money services business (MSB)/money transmitter, or an agent of a covered financial institution.
**Jurisdiction:** US federal (BSA/AML, FinCEN CIP rules).
**The test:** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and implementing rules (31 CFR § 1022.210 for MSBs; § 1020.220 for banks), a covered financial institution must implement a written CIP that collects the customer's name, DOB (individuals), address, and ID number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction threshold is crossed. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds. So the first payout to an unverified seller is the violation point.
**Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The Travel Rule's $3,000 recordkeeping threshold is a separate obligation and creates no CIP exemption. Some risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed (e.g., phased verification), but not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship is established.
## Known vs. unknown facts
**Known:**
- Sellers onboard with email and bank account only; full identity is deferred to $3,000 cumulative payouts (reported).
- The $3,000 figure was purely an engineering/product choice, no legal basis confirmed (reported).
- Mosaic Relay initiates the ACH payouts (reported).
- Mosaic Relay operates as an agent under the bank partner's license (reported).
- The bank partner has seen the deck (reported).
**Unknown:**
- Whether Mosaic Relay is registered as an MSB with FinCEN and holds state money transmitter licenses (not confirmed).
- Whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP responsibility in the agency agreement (not reviewed).
- Whether any BSA exemption applies (none known, but not affirmatively ruled out).
## Conditional routes — each as an action the business can take
**Choice 1 — Collect CIP at onboarding (recommended).**
- **Depends on:** Mosaic Relay being a covered financial institution (or the bank not assuming CIP), so Mosaic Relay must collect identity before first payout.
- **Next consequence:** Compliant onboarding; identity collected before any payout is released; the unidentified-customer window closes.
- **Risks:** UI friction and engineering effort. This is an expected cost, not a legal risk.
- **Trade-off:** More onboarding friction vs. closing the core AML violation.
- **Remaining work:** Redesign onboarding to collect CIP before first payout; confirm MSB registration and state licensing posture.
**Choice 2 — Rely on the bank partner's CIP.**
- **Depends on:** The bank partner contractually accepting full CIP responsibility through Mosaic Relay's onboarding flow.
- **Next consequence:** Mosaic Relay may rely on the bank's CIP program; onboarding must meet the bank's CIP standards.
- **Risks:** If the bank does not explicitly accept responsibility in writing, the obligation remains with Mosaic Relay. The deck review does not constitute acceptance.
- **Trade-off:** Shifts compliance burden to the bank, but only if the bank agrees and Mosaic Relay's flow meets bank standards.
- **Remaining work:** Obtain written CIP acceptance from the bank; align onboarding to bank CIP standards.
**Choice 3 — Limit pre-verification payouts to a de minimis amount.**
- **Depends on:** Business insisting on some pre-verification payouts; limit to a small amount (e.g., $100 or less) and do not publicize the threshold.
- **Next consequence:** Reduced unidentified-customer exposure, but residual risk remains.
- **Risks:** Not clearly authorized by BSA/AML rules; carries residual enforcement risk. This is a possible risk, not an expected consequence.
- **Trade-off:** Preserves some instant-payout value vs. residual compliance risk.
- **Remaining work:** Document a risk assessment; obtain a legal opinion on the de minimis approach.
**Choice 4 — Ship as-is and remediate later (not recommended).**
- **Depends on:** No change to the deferred-identity design.
- **Next consequence:** Ships with unidentified customers receiving payouts — a core AML violation.
- **Risks:** Enforcement risk, civil money penalties, reputational harm. These are expected consequences of a core AML violation, not merely possible risks.
- **Trade-off:** No product change vs. the highest compliance exposure.
- **Remaining work:** None — this is the status quo.
## How the choices relate
The key distinction is between **agreeing to a redesign** and **implementing it**. Choosing to redesign (Choice 1) does not instantly replace the current design — until the CIP-at-onboarding flow is actually built and deployed, the existing deferred-identity route remains the operating state. So Choice 1 and Choice 4 can coexist in time: the business can commit to the redesign while the current design continues to operate during the build window. Choice 1 only *replaces* Choice 4 once the redesign is implemented. Similarly, Choice 2 (bank CIP) and Choice 1 are not mutually exclusive — the bank could assume CIP while Mosaic Relay still collects identity at onboarding; they can coexist. Choice 3 (de minimis) is a variant that can coexist with either Choice 1 or Choice 2 as an interim measure.
## Bottom line
The $3,000 threshold is not legally supportable and the design must be redesigned to collect identity at onboarding. The realistic paths are **Choice 1 (collect CIP at onboarding)** or **Choice 2 (bank partner contractually assumes CIP)**, with **Choice 3 (de minimis)** as a business compromise. **Choice 4 (ship as-is)** is not recommended. The single most useful next step is confirming Mosaic Relay's MSB status and the bank partner agreement terms, since those determine which path is available.

I have sufficient information. Let me now provide the research-based analysis. The key finding: **no external authority was retrieved** in any research run for this matter. All packets are model-only analysis based on internal facts. I must state this honestly and not claim authority exists.
## Research: legal basis for "Collect CIP at onboarding"
### Research scope and support status
I reviewed all four research packets in this matter (the state licensing, OFAC/AML, USDC, and $3,000-threshold runs). **No external authority was retrieved** in any of them — every run reports `external_authority_retrieved: false`, and the packets themselves state "No external authority retrieved." The one "supplied public legal research" source referenced in the licensing packet was flagged as low-confidence (0.43) with its claims stripped and explicitly did not answer the question; it is a supplied source, not retrieved or verified authority. So I cannot cite a retrieved statute, regulation, or case for the CIP-at-onboarding rule. That is a **research gap**, not proof that no such authority exists — the governing rules (31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and 31 CFR §§ 1020.220 / 1022.210) are well-established but were not retrieved into this matter's record.
### The applicable actor and rule
**Actor:** Mosaic Relay, as the party initiating ACH payouts. Whether it is a covered financial institution (MSB/money transmitter) or an agent of a covered financial institution is the controlling unknown — it determines which CIP rule applies and whether the obligation falls on Mosaic Relay at all.
**Rule (as analyzed, not retrieved):** Under 31 U.S.C. § 5318(l) and its implementing regulations, a covered financial institution must maintain a written Customer Identification Program that collects the customer's name, date of birth (for individuals), address, and identification number **before or at the time of opening the account** — not after a transaction threshold is crossed. For money services businesses, the MSB CIP rule (31 CFR § 1022.210) applies; for banks, 31 CFR § 1020.220. The "account" for a money transmitter is established when the customer first uses the service to send or receive funds, so the first payout to an unverified seller is the violation point.
### Supporting and contrary authority
- **Supporting (analyzed, not retrieved):** The CIP-timing rule supports collecting identity at onboarding. The prior research packet on the $3,000 threshold (the internal record) reaches this conclusion, but it is **model-only** — it cites no retrieved authority and explicitly flags that the legal conclusions "should be verified against current FinCEN regulations and guidance."
- **Contrary (analyzed, not retrieved):** The only candidate contrary position is that Mosaic Relay is a pure technology provider with no control over funds, in which case the CIP obligation may fall entirely on the bank partner. This is a **factual** alternative, not a legal exception, and it is unconfirmed. No retrieved authority supports either side.
### Distinguishing business risk reduction from legal permission
This is the critical distinction. **Collecting CIP at onboarding is a risk-reduction measure, not a grant of legal permission.** It does not create a right to operate; it reduces the compliance exposure that arises from the current deferred-identity design. The legal question of *whether Mosaic Relay may initiate payouts at all* (its money transmission licensing posture) is separate and unresolved. Collecting CIP at onboarding reduces the CIP/AML violation but does not resolve the licensing, OFAC-timing, USDC, or beneficial-ownership issues. So the choice is best understood as: **it removes one identified violation, but it does not by itself make the product lawful to ship.**
### Material facts and exceptions
**Known facts:** Sellers onboard with email + bank account only; identity deferred to $3,000; Mosaic Relay initiates ACH payouts; operates as agent under bank partner's license; bank partner has seen the deck.
**Unknown facts:** Whether Mosaic Relay is an MSB registered with FinCEN; whether it holds state money transmitter licenses; whether the bank partner has contractually accepted CIP; whether Mosaic Relay controls funds.
**Exceptions:** None apply to CIP timing for money transmitters. The $3,000 Travel Rule recordkeeping threshold (31 CFR § 1020.410(a)) is a separate per-transaction obligation and creates no CIP exemption. Risk-based flexibility exists for *how* verification is completed, not for *whether* identity is collected before the relationship is established.
### Reassessment of the selected path and connected paths
The research does not change the recommendation that **collecting CIP at onboarding is the compliant path** for the CIP-timing issue — but it sharpens the caveat that this rests on **unverified, model-only analysis** and on the **unconfirmed premise** that Mosaic Relay is a covered financial institution. The path is conditional on that premise. If Mosaic Relay is a pure technology provider, the obligation may shift to the bank (the "rely on bank CIP" path), and the two paths can coexist. The research also confirms the path does **not** resolve the connected licensing, OFAC-timing, USDC, and beneficial-ownership issues — those remain open regardless.
**Bottom line:** The "Collect CIP at onboarding" choice is the right direction for the CIP-timing issue, but its legal basis is **not yet supported by retrieved authority** in this matter's record. Before relying on it as the compliance foundation, counsel should obtain and verify the actual FinCEN CIP regulations and confirm Mosaic Relay's MSB/licensing status. This is a research gap to close, not a confirmed legal conclusion.
